### [First Bill Introduced to Ban Superintelligent AI](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uzoLm4prFzRiuznJt/first-bill-introduced-to-ban-superintelligent-ai)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789034177/lexical_client_uploads/ja5cii8q52ur8mve6n26.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Two days ago, Anthropic researcher Jacob Coxon </span><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/anthropic-researcher-quits-over-out-of-control-ai-fears-707b7628"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">resigned</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2097476196791709843"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">stating</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that AI companies are “racing straight to self-improving superintelligence and gambling with our lives” and that they believe it “could kill us all by the end of the decade”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the same time, momentum is building to change course. These have been two historic weeks in the fight to prevent </span><a href="https://aistatement.com/work/statement-on-ai-extinction-risk"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">human extinction from superintelligence</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, with </span><a href="https://time.com/article/2026/09/08/ban-superintelligence-ai-uk-us-lawmakers/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">three major legislative breakthroughs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for ControlAI and everyone working to keep humans in control.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A little less than two years ago, we set out to inform lawmakers and the public about the extinction risk from superintelligence and help them act on it. Since then, we have directly briefed nearly 400 lawmakers across the US, UK, Canada, and Germany, and over 170 in the </span><a href="https://www.theguardian.com/technology/2025/dec/08/scores-of-uk-parliamentarians-join-call-to-regulate-most-powerful-ai-systems"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">UK</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.cbc.ca/news/politics/canada-superintelligent-ai-development-national-strategy-mark-carney-evan-solomon-9.7222334"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Canada</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> now publicly </span><a href="https://controlai.org/statement"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">back</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> our </span><a href="https://controlai.org/canada-statement/en"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">campaigns</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: the start of an international coalition to prevent superintelligence and keep humanity in control.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This work has led directly to three major legislative breakthroughs in the last two weeks:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ControlAI’s bill to ban superintelligence was </span><a href="https://x.com/ControlAI/status/2097374482197340538"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">introduced</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in the UK Parliament by Alex Sobel MP, the first bill of this kind to be introduced in any legislature around the world.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Senator Bernie Sanders and Representative Greg Casar have </span><a href="https://www.sanders.senate.gov/press-releases/news-sanders-casar-introduce-legislation-to-ban-artificial-superintelligence-and-temporarily-pause-advanced-ai-development/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">announced</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a US bill to ban superintelligence, which ControlAI </span><a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/wp-intelligence/ai-tech-brief/2026/09/03/ai-tech-brief-tech-contracting-shake-up/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">consulted</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lord Clement-Jones </span><a href="https://www.bbc.co.uk/news/articles/cn9wv80j9w9o"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">introduced</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in the UK legislature an amendment drafted by ControlAI that would establish an emergency AI kill switch for the government.</span></li></ol><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The First Bill to Ban Superintelligent AI Introduced in Any Legislature</span></h1><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789034231/lexical_client_uploads/erbyakivt54bxampk4ni.png" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ControlAI team members heading into Parliament on Tuesday to watch it happen.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On September 8, </span><a href="https://www.politicshome.com/opinion/article/government-block-development-superintelligent-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alex Sobel</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> MP </span><a href="https://www.thetimes.com/business/technology/article/ai-godfather-catastrophic-end-humanity-legislation-npzvb7hxp"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">introduced</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ControlAI’s UK </span><a href="https://controlai.org/uk-asi-bill"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Artificial Superintelligence Security Bill</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on the floor of the UK House of Commons, with cross-party support.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This bill, first </span><a href="https://x.com/ControlAI/status/2058501906268405947"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">announced</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in May, </span><a href="https://www.theguardian.com/technology/2026/sep/09/ai-superintelligence-risks-warnings-scientists-politicians"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">prohibits the development of superintelligence</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to address the national security risk it poses, while ensuring the UK can still pursue its AI ambitions for economic prosperity and defense.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A domestic ban protects the UK from threats on its own soil, but not from the development of superintelligence abroad. No matter who builds ASI, or where they build it, we are all in danger: truly addressing the threat requires a worldwide prohibition on ASI development. The bill recognizes this, requiring the UK government to work with other countries to negotiate an international agreement prohibiting superintelligence worldwide.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is feasible to detect and deter superintelligence development. It is a large-scale industrial process, akin to uranium enrichment. It requires massive data centers, visible from orbit and detectable through thermal imaging satellites, filled with state-of-the-art AI chips produced through narrow supply chains that can be restricted. Specific markers also help distinguish superintelligence development from most AI development, giving governments the means to establish a “trust, but verify” regime.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And momentum is growing on both sides of the Atlantic.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sanders and Casar Announce the First US Bill to Ban Superintelligent AI</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Last week, Senator Bernie Sanders and Representative Greg Casar </span><a href="https://www.sanders.senate.gov/press-releases/news-sanders-casar-introduce-legislation-to-ban-artificial-superintelligence-and-temporarily-pause-advanced-ai-development/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">announced the first US bill to ban superintelligence</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This announcement comes after Representatives Nathaniel Moran and Ted Lieu </span><a href="https://lieu.house.gov/media-center/press-releases/reps-lieu-and-moran-introduce-bill-require-kill-switch-ai-systems-can"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">introduced</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the </span><a href="https://www.congress.gov/bill/119th-congress/house-bill/9917/text"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Kill Switch Act</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in July, </span><a href="https://www.the-independent.com/tech/openai-ai-kill-switch-chatgpt-bill-b3020779.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">which we were happy to endorse</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As reported by the Washington Post, ControlAI </span><a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/wp-intelligence/ai-tech-brief/2026/09/03/ai-tech-brief-tech-contracting-shake-up/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">consulted on the Sanders-Casar framework</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Our wider US advocacy has included </span><a href="https://www.wired.com/story/uk-lawmakers-are-scrambling-in-response-to-ai-summer-of-chaos/?utm_social-type=owned"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">briefing over 200 congressional offices</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and holding over 20 meetings directly with members of Congress in a little over a year.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is fantastic that the first bill in the US banning superintelligence recognizes that any national ban must be coupled with an effort to prevent superintelligence development worldwide. This is crucial for any framework on preventing superintelligence.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The framework also proposes a temporary pause on advanced AI development. Our own approach is narrower: prohibit the development of superintelligence specifically, and treat a specific subset of AI capabilities as superintelligence precursors to be monitored and restricted.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We look forward to seeing how the full bill balances these goals. In the meantime, you can read our </span><a href="https://drafts.controlai.org/asi-ban-bill"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">US Ban ASI Bill discussion draft</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lord Clement-Jones Introduces an Emergency AI Kill Switch in the UK</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Last week, on September 1, Lord Clement-Jones </span><a href="https://www.bbc.co.uk/news/articles/cn9wv80j9w9o"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">put forward</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ControlAI’s </span><a href="https://blog.controlai.org/p/we-need-an-ai-kill-switch"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">kill switch amendment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to the UK’s </span><a href="https://bills.parliament.uk/bills/4035"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cyber Security and Resilience Bill</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, marking the second time ControlAI has worked with UK parliamentarians to advance such a measure in this bill, after Alex Sobel </span><a href="https://www.telegraph.co.uk/business/2026/05/16/mps-demand-ai-kill-switch-to-protect-british-lives/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">introduced</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> another </span><a href="https://bills.parliament.uk/bills/4035/stages/20525/amendments/10034477"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">kill switch amendment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> earlier this year.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://bills.parliament.uk/publications/67506/documents/8704"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">amendment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> recognizes superintelligence as a national security threat, and provides a stopgap measure allowing the government to shut down data centers and AI systems deployed at scale in the UK in case AIs are threatening national security.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An AI kill switch is a common-sense measure that governments should put in place, though it will not be sufficient if we do reach superintelligence, as such systems would undermine any attempt to shut them down.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What Comes Next</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Two years ago, no legislature in the world had seen a bill to ban superintelligence. Now one has been introduced, with a second bill announced.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What these bills and amendments do is set out measures countries can adopt domestically, and set the path for them to negotiate international deals to enforce them around the world. And importantly, they open up the public conversation we need to have to get a ban on superintelligence in place.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each national breakthrough makes getting another country on board easier, and each of them builds the international coalition we need.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This was achieved with a small team, in less than two years. The approach is straightforward, repeatable, and compounds via a public, growing coalition of lawmakers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The work is only beginning. We will keep working with lawmakers across parties and countries to keep humanity in control.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uzoLm4prFzRiuznJt/first-bill-introduced-to-ban-superintelligent-ai#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Doom as a bad method not a utopia trade-off](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pDyLMRoi2BDq34rFe/doom-as-a-bad-method-not-a-utopia-trade-off)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Advanced AI is generally expected to have some very high variance outcomes—it might herald everything good, it might destroy humanity. For instance, here are 800 random AI researchers’ expectations about how good the future is, lined up:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/pDyLMRoi2BDq34rFe/erypdqcjn9n6nstm4mwz" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From&nbsp;</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2401.02843v1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">my 2023 survey</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As you can see, most AI researchers put a serious chunk of probability on very different overall outcomes: maybe doom, maybe utopia. This is common. Most people I know who think there is a serious chance of the destruction of humanity from AI also believe that if humanity isn’t destroyed, things might be insanely good.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I often hear people talk as if this means we are in a trade-off where the question is whether the good outweighs the bad. For instance, they look at the people above who think there’s a 10% chance of extinction and a 30% chance of utopia and round this off to ‘net positive on AI’.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That seems like a kind of wild error. Like considering yourself optimistic regarding driving at 200mph to your new job if you think there’s only a 10% chance you’ll die in a fiery crash on the way there, and a 30% chance this job will radically improve your life.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The things you should be comparing are driving at 200mph and driving at a normal speed! The things you should be comparing are attempting to attain advanced AI by the current route, and by other routes!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can debate whether all the other routes are bad or impossible somehow, for instance if constraining projects that risk loss of human control risks sending humanity into an irrecoverable ruin. But I don’t think having ruled out such things is why people are usually thinking in trade-off terms.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rather I think this error comes from a few things:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It being simpler to think of ‘pros vs. cons’ and the topic being too abstract for people to intuitively notice that they are comparing pros of a long term outcome vs. cons of the&nbsp;</span><a href="https://worldspiritsockpuppet.substack.com/p/the-first-future-and-the-best-future?utm_source=publication-search"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">first route</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;there we have noticed</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sloppiness about talking about P(doom). Saying ‘P(doom)’ encourages thinking as if ‘AI’ implies a particular chance of ‘doom’. We should more accurately think about ‘p(doom|’such and such route’), e.g. P(doom|advanced AI from scaling up LLMs). People usually mean ‘P(doom|current trajectory)’ with some ambiguity about whether the current trajectory includes our own actions.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://worldspiritsockpuppet.substack.com/p/ai-is-an-abundance-of-choice-not"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thinking of AI as one scalar</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;vs. a bunch of different things we could build</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you are bullish on some kind of advanced AI utopia, you should generally be&nbsp;</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">less</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">keen to try to achieve it via a careless route that leaves you at high risk of dying and losing it on the way there.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pDyLMRoi2BDq34rFe/doom-as-a-bad-method-not-a-utopia-trade-off#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [What the Pro-Democracy Movement Knows About Quitting in Protest](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/grzX6NB2JcZDvSRJ2/what-the-pro-democracy-movement-knows-about-quitting-in)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tl;dr</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I saw </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6j3kBHdowGLCeqobg"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kabir's post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LessWrong</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and thought the argument could be strengthened by a framework from the pro-democracy field, which sorts defections into breaking (leave, visibly and publicly) and binding (stay and work from the inside), each further divided divided into speaking, acting, and standing in the way. The best data I know of on what moves institutions during democratic backsliding (</span><a href="https://jonathanpinckney.com/publication/pillars_paper/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pinckney &amp; Trilling, 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) says: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Noncooperation by insiders succeeded ~2x as often as speaking out (67% vs 33%).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Loyalty shifts are rare (23 out of 140 chances). When they happened, quiet outreach to insiders moved them ~39% of the time; public protest did worse than no campaign at all.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Below I'll attempt to translate this to the frontier labs, with the obvious caveat that a lab is not a regime. The short version: refusing the specific work while staying is the higher-success action in their data, and staying in the institution to quietly move colleagues is closer to what works than either resigning or striking.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Background</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I've spent 15 years in political campaigns and organizing, with training and experience in applying the lessons of pro-democracy movements abroad to the US. For the past year, I have attempted to scope a few projects that could be run in the US (one of them is the project I now help lead). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I went deep on the concept of "defections," since Pinckney and Trilling's paper shows they are one of the most effective tactics in pro-democracy organizing. The concepts of breaking and binding were </span><a href="https://horizonsproject.us/wp-content/uploads/2025/09/Shifting-Pillar-Loyalties.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">coined by</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Adam Fefer in his August 2025 guide </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Shifting Pillar Loyalties.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, I help lead an election defense project called Hold the Line that equips people with evidence-based, nonpartisan frameworks to help them understand threats to democratic institutions; organize effective collective action; and defend free, fair, and safe elections. It enables individuals to organize democracy defense teams in their own communities, wherever they are.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Definitions</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fefer defines breaking as visibly and publicly removing yourself from the group or institution you were part of, and binding as when you try to change it from the inside, through back-channel outreach, subtle persuasion, or by preventing someone from taking your place. Both matter, and the choice depends on where you are in the institution. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The guide also sorts the actions available to you into speaking (publicly or privately), acting (protest, organizing, lawsuits), and standing in the way (noncooperation, refusing a task, work stoppages).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's a way to visualize it:</span></p><table class="editor-table table-alignment-center"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Speaking</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Acting</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Standing in the way</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Breaking</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Resign with a public letter</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Leave and organize, litigate</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Leave and take the team with you</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Binding</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dissent internally on the record</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Organize colleagues, build a back channel</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Refuse a specific task, don't let a "yes person" take your seat</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fefer's guide has a table of real world cases from anti-authoritarian movements, including here in the US and in Poland, that may be useful to lab employees. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we were to analyze recent frontier lab employee actions, Jacob Coxon's resignation is breaking + speaking. Kabir's proposal to refuse the work and let them fire you is binding + standing in the way.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Digging deeper into the data</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What was most surprising to me was Pinckney and Trilling's finding on quiet outreach. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When a campaign's main strategy toward a pillar of power was quiet outreach, that pillar moved toward democracy about 39% of the time. When the main strategy was pressure (physical or verbal protest), campaigns did worse than cases with no campaign at all. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the success of what actors in those pillars did, noncooperation had the highest success rate (67%) vs. verbal protest (33%). The authors find that when actors in the pillars "merely speak out, there is minimal impact." Success is more likely when they use their position to stop cooperation and directly challenge the source of backsliding. The clear caveat is that loyalty shifts are rare and it's a small sample size and they suggest correlation only.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What may be instructive to a frontier lab employee</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think using the table above could be instructive to frontier lab employees. For instance, what made Coxon's departure worth more than a resignation is that it pulled a public </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6j3kBHdowGLCeqobg"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">statement from Evan Hubinger</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, who is still on the inside.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On binding, if an employee is asked to do something unethical, they could do the following: ask for orders in writing, run it past legal, don't do work outside of their contract, be unavailable if something is truly unethical or immoral, document everything, and refuse openly at the point they are called on to make a decision. habryka is right that you very well could be sidelined and the lab will wait for you to make a mistake. (To be clear: Nothing here is legal advice, and if any of this could touch your employment, talk to a lawyer first.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My opinion is that there could be a strategy where ex-lab employees organize into affinity or support groups (similar to what we saw after DOGE eliminated most of USAID's staff) that then organize their connections at the frontier labs. This would likely be the strongest method to start doing relational organizing. I'm not sure if anyone is working on this or it's been proposed, but I think it could be an interesting mandate for someone at an AI safety organization or for an ex-lab employee to spin up a project of their own.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For the AI safety community, I think this research could be very useful in thinking through the benefits of relational organizing (quiet outreach) vs. protest and putting pressure on the labs from the outside. The strategy with the best record for shifting insider loyalty was the somewhat boring relational work. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'd be really curious to hear from any lab employees on if this framework is useful and how likely they'd be to work with an outside entity to enact some of this strategy if it were well organized and structured.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI use: I used AI to pull data from papers I previously read as well as to tighten up the structure of this post. As this is my first post, I wanted to write in the structure of  LessWrong, which was unfamiliar to me. Mistakes are mine alone. Eager for feedback!</span></i></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sources</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adam Fefer, </span><a href="https://horizonsproject.us/wp-content/uploads/2025/09/Shifting-Pillar-Loyalties.pdf"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Shifting Pillar Loyalties: A Guide for the Pro-Democracy Community</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Horizons Project / SNF Agora Institute, August 2025</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jonathan Pinckney &amp; Claire Trilling, "Breaking Down Pillars of Support for Democratic Backsliding," </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mobilization</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (2024): </span><a href="https://jonathanpinckney.com/publication/pillars_paper/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">summary</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.researchgate.net/publication/371371333_Breaking_Down_Pillars_of_Support_For_Democratic_Backsliding"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">preprint PDF</span></a></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kabir Kumar, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6j3kBHdowGLCeqobg"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Dear God, Please Do Not Resign In Protest"</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, LessWrong, Sept 7, 2026 (the Hubinger statement is quoted in Connor Williams' comment)</span></li></ul><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/grzX6NB2JcZDvSRJ2/what-the-pro-democracy-movement-knows-about-quitting-in#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Categorical taboos are much better than threshold taboos: neuralese edition](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xPkmfsZ3qx4nrAco7/categorical-taboos-are-much-better-than-threshold-taboos)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think what's going on in the “Does Astra use neuralese?” debate is that there's an important sense in which models *</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">already</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">* do self-communication in neuralese: between each layer in the forward pass, the attention stream is already very hard to interpret, and clearly not in natural language.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yet CoT monitorability is still a big deal and it'd be bad if all self-communication from models are no longer in natural language. So there have been two different proposed definitions</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwjgj1coa7wd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwjgj1coa7wd">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of what is "true" neuralese:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(My preferred) </span><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/what-is-neuralese"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">categorical definition</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Since natural language currently gates recurrence in the standard transformer+CoT loop, having recurrence in neuralese is the natural category for whether something counts as "true" neuralese.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref92fyt7n0fqo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn92fyt7n0fqo">[2]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The threshold definition. Total number X of serial steps before something appears in natural language. True neuralese counts as going above X. I think most technical experts who studied this issue, including many people at companies, prefer definition #2.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are complicated technical arguments on both sides, but I think technical experts overall prefer #2 because they think it's more causally relevant (there’s nothing inherently more difficult about monitoring a 128-layer model looped 8x than monitoring a 1024-layer model), have less weird edge cases, etc. Whereas I (moderately) prefer #1 because I think definition #1 is conceptually cleaner and easier to enforce in practice.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But regardless of your preferred technical definition, an interesting sociological observation is that it appears (from reading their comments) that OpenAI (safety?) people appears to have taken definition #2 as gospel and fully reified it. Furthermore, if you take definition #2 as your only definition, it's impossible to defect against the no-neuralese taboo unless you specifically hit above the threshold (also as two side notes: 1) I'm not sure if either OpenAI or the safety community has ever clearly defined a threshold that people have agreed upon and 2) the number of layers for frontier models is not public).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Like from that perspective, adding recurrence a bunch is not obviously defecting more than adding more layers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Though in my opinion, trying to fight against adding more layers is both less politically feasible and less useful (since mostly companies would only agree to a layer threshold because it's too technically hard to scale by adding more layers anyway).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So if we end up agreeing on definition #2 as the only definition we're talking about about re: neuralese, we can't say people are defecting unless they go above X layers (where, again, X has never been set, and also the specific number of layers is unknown).</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 41.99361542682819%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789066538/lexical_client_uploads/ygpgogrfg2axyomkmvvl.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Speed limits are often pushed in the best of times, but a speed limit without a number isn't a limit at all.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">__</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The definition above might be a bit overly technical, as well as philosophical and hard to understand, even for LessWrong, so maybe it’d be helpful to consider two (okay but not amazing) analogies for similar situations: the nuclear weapons taboo, and doping.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The nuclear weapons taboo is one where there’s an important sense that the categorical taboo makes no causally relevant sense: the smallest tactical nuclear bombs have a lower yield than the biggest conventional bombs. So why have a taboo against nukes in war, rather than against bomb sizes? Well, as Thomas Schelling has written about multiple times, the categorical line is necessary because a yield-based definition has no focal point: once you allow “tactical” nuclear use, there’s no natural place to stop. So a combination of scholars, activists, and farseeing world leaders managed to converge on the categorical definition. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For sports doping in contrast, a categorical definition is obviously superior in spirit but practically impossible to enforce in practice. The tests all have error rates and measurement issues, and besides, often the things that people dope with are naturally present in variable amounts in athletes' bodies. So </span><a href="https://www.wada-ama.org/en"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">WADA</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ended up using the categorical definition as the official one, but set various numerical thresholds to define doping in practice. But people generally understand that the numerical thresholds are the regulatory device, as a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">proxy</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for the underlying thing we care about. So when Lance Armstrong bragged that he “never failed a test”, people didn’t consider this as definitional evidence that he didn’t dope. And regulators eventually got him on teammates’ testimonies even when he never ended up failing a test.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that as a matter of regulation, it might well be the case that for practical reasons, you have to have threshold-based laws or regulations rather than categorical ones. And I think it’s fine to say that we shouldn’t legally punish anyone for breaking the spirit of the law if they didn’t break the letter, especially in somewhat ambiguous domains.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrn49stk8be9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrn49stk8be9">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But as a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">social</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">societal</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> matter, we absolutely should be comfortable with critiquing and punishing people and institutions who break the spirit of norms even if they didn’t break the letter. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Two more points: OpenAI’s defenses include that the total serial depth of Astra’s computation </span><a href="https://x.com/merettm/status/2095023204993490967"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">isn’t very high</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and also that we shouldn’t kick off a race to </span><a href="https://x.com/MicahCarroll/status/2095023282051563835"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">unmonitorable neuralese by talking about it as a done deal.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I mostly agree with these arguments as stated (though the first one has some </span><a href="https://x.com/RyanGreenblatt/status/2095041232145981897"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">very important nuances</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), but they need to be taken in the context of breaking a taboo. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is true even if you have quantitative and categorical defenses that you aren’t breaking the taboo as much as is maximally bad. In the nuclear case, if a country uses a small tactical nuclear bomb on the battlefield defensively, I agree it’s not nearly as bad as using bigger bombs, attacking civilian populations, or escalating to all-out nuclear war. Indeed, if I’m a policy scientist working on nuclear deterrence when that happens, I’d absolutely be trying my hardest to either attempt to reinstate the nuclear weapons taboo or create a new categorical one (e.g. no nukes on civilians). But it’s still breaking a taboo to use nukes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the doping case, I agree that somebody who only dopes in training is not as bad as somebody who dopes both in training and during the race itself. But it’s still bad to dope!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In a broad sense, categorical taboos are much better than threshold-based taboos, and precise numerical threshold-based taboos better than ambiguous ones. The last one is barely a taboo at all.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I recommend people thinking about which norms and taboos to adopt and shape be significantly more mindful of the relevant practical complexity in understanding and enforcing said norms, both in general and in the neuralese case. </span></p><p><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwjgj1coa7wd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefwjgj1coa7wd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People have also alluded to a third definition, which is that the thing we “actually” care about is CoT monitorability so we should define neuralese as the obverse of CoT monitorability. Imo if you try to think it through, this consequentialist definition is even more unnatural and cursed. We do not have a crisp enough conceptual definition of moniterability, nor do we have sufficient numerical evaluations and thresholds for what counts as “sufficiently moniterable.”  </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn92fyt7n0fqo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref92fyt7n0fqo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is also closest to the original AI 2027 definition, which afaik is what kick-started most public conversations about neuralese in technical circles. Because it came out 1.5 years ago, of course the wording and definitions are slightly less precise than current discussion.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrn49stk8be9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrn49stk8be9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Though again I will point out that even steelmanning this perspective is somewhat putting the cart before the horse here, since, again, no numerical thresholds have yet been set.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xPkmfsZ3qx4nrAco7/categorical-taboos-are-much-better-than-threshold-taboos#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The Geometry of Nonergodic Composition](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JfJ4WTRHmooPBWRFv/the-geometry-of-nonergodic-composition)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><em>Crossposted from <a href="https://belief-updates.pub/nonergodic-geometry/">Belief Updates</a>, the Simplex blog, where several of the figures are interactive. By Kyle J. Ray, Paul M. Riechers, and Adam S. Shai (Simplex, Astera Institute).</em></p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/SocialPreview/rphwgivk5uzv9p9ohieu" /></p>
<p><em>Telescoping cones recovered by linear regression from a transformer’s residual-stream activations. Each component grows and shrinks in accordance with in-context evidence.</em></p>
<h2>Introduction</h2>
<p>Perhaps the defining feature of LLM pretraining data is its heterogeneity. The training corpus spans not only the collected and varied textual works output by the whole of humanity, but also those generated by machines, data collection devices, and more. Such a large and varied corpus is often appealed to as an explanation for the abilities of modern LLMs<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span>. But <strong>the statistical structure of data created by a diverse set of generators also implies a particular computational structure for the next token prediction task, and, as we will see, for the geometric arrangement of the internal activations in LLMs.</strong></p>
<p>In order to understand the structure of the next token prediction task over data generated from many different sources, and its implications for the geometric structure of activations in neural networks, we will:</p>
<ul>
<li>Start by introducing the concept of <strong>nonergodicity</strong>, which is an important property of LLM training data. Nonergodicity formalizes the notion of a generator of data made of many sources.</li>
<li>Derive the belief geometry that the prediction task over such data implies: per-source belief geometries whose magnitude scales in accordance with how strongly the context supports each component. This gives rise to the telescoping geometric structures shown in this post, and to <strong>feature geometry that is</strong> <strong>eventually sparse and multi-dimensional.</strong></li>
<li>Treat this geometry as a falsifiable prediction. If a network represents beliefs linearly, as we have found before[5], the geometry of its activations should take this form. By using data with known mixture structure and known per-component geometry, we are able to predict and then observe a transformer building that geometry.</li>
</ul>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789058470/lexical_client_uploads/g8hxaf6svk9zniu3aydn.png" /></p>
<p>We are excited about these results because nonergodicity is a fundamental statistical aspect of real pretraining data. It is, in some sense, the structure that makes in-context learning both necessary and powerful. [6] argued that when pretraining data is a mixture of latent generative concepts, in-context learning should be thought of as the model implicitly performing Bayesian inference over those concepts. In [7] and [5], we showed that next-token pretraining forces this kind of inference even within a single concept. Nonergodic data calls for both at once. Here, we investigate the specific computational and geometric implications of this type of hierarchical inference.</p>
<h2>LLM Training Data is Nonergodic</h2>
<p>Consider a natural language sequence beginning with <code>Do not make…</code>. This ambiguous opening reveals little about the source.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788994671/lexical_client_uploads/n0zcfmgzsybkzb7nslza.png" /></p>
<p>These four completions share a prefix, but they do not share a future. Note how each continuation gives information about the generator of that data sample, in this case, a Redditor, an instruction manual, etc. These different sources create token sequences with different correlation structure, which is revealed through additional context. The language of a document, the genre of a story, and the identity of a speaker are all initial choices that jointly constrain the subsequent tokens. In the language of stochastic processes, these situations correspond to <strong>nonergodic compositions</strong>: mixtures of distinct generative processes, where the identity of the active process is fixed at the start of each sequence generation and never revisited. Much of our previous work has dealt with inference over a world model composed of a single generator; here we extend the discussion to include the meta process of inference about <em>which</em> of multiple generators in the world we should be modeling at all.</p>
<p>In the rest of this post, we will explain how the theory predicts a telescoping geometric structure for beliefs over this kind of data, and show some initial results consistent with the fact that transformers represent that geometry in their activations when trained on nonergodic data.</p>
<h3>Two coins: the simplest example of a nonergodic process</h3>
<p>In order to understand inference over such nonergodic data sources, we will start with the simple example of data generated from one of two coins. Imagine you know that I have two coins, coin  and coin , each of different fixed biases. I secretly choose one at random and start flipping it. You see only the outcomes: <code>H, T, H, H, T, H, H,…</code>. Your task is to predict the next heads or tails. To do that, it would be useful if you could figure out if it was coin  or coin  that was responsible for the flips you’ve seen so far. At first, you have no idea which coin is being flipped, and all you have is your prior: “it could just as easily be either coin”. But as flips accumulate, the frequency of heads tilts toward one of the two biases, and you will become more confident about which of the coins is active. That process is the process of sequentially updating your posterior to a strong belief about the world: “I’m pretty sure I know which coin it is.”</p>
<p>This is the simplest nonergodic composition; the coins are stand-ins for more generic <em>ergodic components<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span></em> that may themselves carry nontrivial latent structure. We will tackle that case momentarily, but here we have two memoryless<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span> components. The only memory is a hidden “which coin” latent that is set once for each sequence generation and never changed. This choice is hidden because you never see the initial selection directly, but Bayesian inference eventually resolves your uncertainty from observation statistics alone.</p>
<p>Together, the two coins can be thought of as one generator with two hidden states and no way to move between them. Once you know that one of the coins generated the sequence, there is nothing more to know, so your knowledge of the system is fully determined by your belief about which coin is generating the data, which is a point on a segment that slides toward one end as evidence accumulates.</p>
<p>On <a href="https://belief-updates.pub/nonergodic-geometry/">the original post</a> you can try this yourself: flip as many times as you like to gather evidence, set your belief about which coin is responsible for generating the data, and then reveal the Bayes-optimal posterior and the coin.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789058471/lexical_client_uploads/htti6vtsgail2kba81l7.png" /></p>
<p><em><a href="https://belief-updates.pub/nonergodic-geometry/">Interactive version on the original post</a>.</em></p>
<p>For these coins, the optimal Bayesian posterior can simply be written down given a sequence of observations; the counts of heads and tails are all it needs (you might remember this from your statistics class). In our case, we have the two coins,  and , with biases  and  and a prior  over which one is active. After  heads and  tails, the posterior on coin  is:</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788994672/lexical_client_uploads/oj949z9j3rnhezblxopv.png" /></p>
<p>The form of this equation shows one of the fundamental lessons of this post. When formally answering the question “what is the probability that coin A generated the sequence?”<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-4">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span> the numerator only depends on information about coin A: coin A’s own likelihood times coin A’s prior. It notably does not depend on information about coin B! The denominator, in contrast, normalizes this numerator by a sum that depends on both coins, and thus couples the belief in coin A with information about both coins. We will see that the form of this belief update, containing a part that has to do with each component independently, and then normalized by a part that has to do with all components, is general.</p>
<p>For the case of the coins, the order of the flips doesn’t matter at all. This is not general, and is atypical of the real world. Most environments that we need to identify have sequential structure. I not you kid. Sorry, rather: I kid you not. Order matters.</p>
<p>In the more general case, the identity of a source lives in the detailed correlation structure of how its tokens follow one another, not just in counts of tokens. A Reddit thread, for example, has many <em>hidden</em> states — what account has replied and what was said are directly observable, but not whether the person behind the account is hungry, or tired. Once components have internal structure, just counting current symbols is no longer enough. We need the general answer to the question the coins raised: what, exactly, must you remember about the past in order to best predict the future? The answer to this is the belief state.</p>
<h2>Nonergodic Generators of Data and the Task of Prediction over them</h2>
<p>To concretize this into a falsifiable theory, we will need to formalize a general notion of a generator of data composed of many different sources. Each source should have its own internal latent structure, and should generate sequences of tokens. In addition, multiple sources need to be able to be composed in such a way that is consistent with the notion of one source being active, or another source, but not more than one simultaneously.</p>
<p>In the following section, we quickly review the mathematical structure of Hidden Markov Models (HMMs) as latent generators of token sequences, the task of prediction over those sequences, and the corresponding belief geometry associated with that prediction task<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-5">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span>. This section is all a review of our earlier work [5], but is necessary to get to the section “Nonergodicity, Prediction, and Telescoping Geometry!” where we use HMMs as building blocks for nonergodic composition of generators, and study the geometric structure of prediction over those.</p>
<h3>HMMs as Latent Generators of Token Sequences</h3>
<p>We are trying to capture the situation relevant to the task of prediction over sequences of data, especially when the data is generated by processes that are hidden to the predictor. As in our earlier work, we will use the framework of Hidden Markov Models (HMMs) as our fundamental generator component.</p>
<p>An HMM has a set of hidden states, , and emits tokens  from a vocabulary . Its dynamics are given by one transition operator per token, , whose entry  is the probability that the generator moves from hidden state  to hidden state  <em>and</em> emits  as it does so. These operators define both how the hidden states move and also how the state dynamics relate to token emissions. You may remember Mess3, the 3-state HMM shown below on the right, from our earlier work.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789058472/lexical_client_uploads/ldmzac2u5seduuvjj72c.png" /></p>
<p>Natural language is of course more complicated than these examples, but notably any stochastic process<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-6">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-6">[6]</a></sup>
    </span> can be generated by some HMM.</p>
<h3>The Task of Prediction and Belief State Geometry</h3>
<p>Despite the name (GPT stands for&nbsp;<em>generative</em>&nbsp;pretrained transformer), transformers are actually (pre)trained to&nbsp;<em>predict</em>, not generate. A predictor observing sequences of tokens and trying to predict the next token cannot directly see the hidden state of the generator. What it can do is keep a belief , a probability distribution over the hidden states, and update it with each token. As discussed in our previous work, an optimal predictor will update its belief, upon seeing a token , from  to , according to Bayes’ Rule.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788994674/lexical_client_uploads/pdkp3h8qrwi38hs28pcs.png" /></p>
<p>These <strong>belief states</strong>  are vectors that live in a probability simplex. The set of belief states that are reachable from the sequences a generator creates thus has a geometry, the <strong>belief state geometry</strong>. For instance, in the case of Mess3, there are an infinite number of distinct belief states, that arrange themselves in the probability simplex as a fractal.</p>
<p>Importantly, the information a belief state contains is everything the past tells you about the future; it is the general answer to the question the coins raised. For a coin the belief over its single state is trivially the number one, which is why counting heads and tails was all there was to do. The posterior over <em>which coin</em> was a belief of a different kind, a belief about which generator is active. As we will now see, in general a predictor has to carry both types of information.</p>
<h2>Nonergodicity, Prediction, and Telescoping Geometry!</h2>
<p>We now have all the pieces needed to create a generator composed of multiple sources/components. The high level approach will be to design a single HMM whose hidden states are the hidden states of all the components put together, and whose dynamics never move between components. The coin game from earlier is a simple example of this: pick a coin, then generate a sequence using only that coin. After we have an HMM that generates nonergodic data, we will figure out the geometric consequences for prediction.</p>
<h2>Nonergodic Composition</h2>
<p>The mathematical move to create generators of nonergodic data, called <strong>nonergodic composition</strong>, will be to compose component HMMs via the direct sum. We find that it is often helpful to see both the general theory and an example to keep intuition grounded, so we give both below, one after the other.</p>
<p><strong>General theory.</strong> Given  component HMMs , the <strong>nonergodic composition</strong> is a single HMM  whose token-labeled transition matrices are the direct sum of the components’ matrices :</p>
<p></p>
<p>The block-diagonal structure is the key property: since the off-diagonal blocks are zero, a state in block  can never transition to block . The process is permanently confined to whichever block it starts in <span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-7">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-7">[7]</a></sup>
    </span>.</p>
<p>We also need to compose the initial states, . Each sums to 1 within its own component, but not across components. So to compose them we need to choose a weight for each component,  with , which is the prior probability that component  is the one generating the data. The initial state vector of the composition is then the concatenation of the components’ initial vectors, each scaled by the weight on its component,</p>
<p></p>
<p><strong>Mess3 example.</strong> Let us consider the nonergodic composition of two Mess3 generators, each acting as a distinct source of token sequences. We will call them Component  and Component . Each Mess3 will have different hyperparameter settings, as shown below. To make a single generator out of these components, in which every sequence is generated either by  or by  with 50/50 probability, we arrange the transition matrices of the two components in block-diagonal form<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-8">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-8">[8]</a></sup>
    </span>.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788994675/lexical_client_uploads/lpj6cq9b60ygz3h6e0gs.png" /></p>
<p>This composite HMM is another HMM, a latent generator of sequences of tokens. Note that because the transition probabilities associated with one component always have zero probability of transition to any state in the other component (the off-diagonal terms are all zero by construction), it is impossible for the generator to move between components, once it has started in one.</p>
<h3>Belief Geometry over Nonergodic Data</h3>
<p>Next, we apply the belief update rule to such a composition of ergodic components. We will see that <strong>while the generator is permanently confined to whichever block it starts in, an observer’s guess about which component is active is not</strong> [8]. Like guessing the hidden coin from a sequence: the true coin is always the same, but as flips accumulate you change your belief about which one it is. In the belief geometry, this ends up coupling geometric structures associated with each component in a particular way.</p>
<p>The belief updating rule is the same as for a single component HMM,</p>
<p></p>
<p>but now both the initial state and the transition operators have block structure:</p>
<p></p>
<p>Let’s take a look at the belief state after a single token emission. Because the off-diagonal blocks of  are zero, the numerator of the belief update acts block by block:</p>
<p></p>
<p>Each component’s initial belief gets multiplied by its own operator, as if it were the only generator. The denominator of the belief update is a normalization, which sums over all entries of the numerator, and thus couples the belief updating across the components by a scalar. A small bit of algebra<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-9">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-9">[9]</a></sup>
    </span> shows that we are again left with a concatenation of per component beliefs each scaled by a scalar  with . The resulting belief state, and indeed all reachable belief states (due to the recursive nature of belief updating), can be expressed this way. We can always decompose a belief as</p>
<p></p>
<p>Because of this, our interpretation of the initial state carries over to all belief states, with the mixture prior  becoming a per-component mixture posterior . In short: t<strong>he belief is a distribution over all components’ hidden states that can always be expressed in terms of the probability</strong>  <strong>that the predictor puts on component</strong> <strong>, and the belief</strong>  <strong>over the states of component</strong> <strong>, conditioned on being in that component.</strong></p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789058472/lexical_client_uploads/mja0f6unlno3bqdiqdcn.png" /></p>
<p><em><a href="https://belief-updates.pub/nonergodic-geometry/">Interactive version on the original post</a>.</em></p>
<p>From this we can see something important about the belief geometry. The beliefs of a nonergodic composition live in a simplex whose dimension is set by the total number of hidden states across all the components. For our two 3-state HMMs, that is 6 states, so the 5-simplex. From that 5 dimensional space, we can project the belief onto the coordinates of any single component, giving . This is a point in a simplex, <strong>but shrunk toward the origin by the weight</strong> <strong>.</strong> The  are not independent from each other: they sum to one. So, as the belief puts more weight on one component, its simplex grows in magnitude, and the others shrink towards the origin. Thus, the projection gives the belief geometry a telescoping effect. Above, we show where the belief vectors can live when looking at this projection for two arbitrary 3-state HMMs, at . The specific fractals for a nonergodic composition of two Mess3s appear in the next figure.</p>
<p>The result is that components that explain the observed data well accrue weight; components that don’t, lose it. Belief updating over such a composition has a characteristic signature: <strong>eventually sparse multi-dimensional features</strong>. Early in context, several components carry non-negligible weight ; as we see more tokens and evidence accumulates we expect  for the true component , and the geometry to collapse onto the active block only.</p>
<h2>Does this geometry show up in trained models?</h2>
<p>The framework above predicts a specific geometric structure for the belief geometry associated with prediction on nonergodic token sequences. When a transformer is trained on next-token prediction over such data, can we find that geometry in its activations?</p>
<p>Here we show our initial positive results. To test this in a transformer we use a nonergodic composition of two Mess3 generators. Mess3’s belief states form a fractal that fills the simplex, so the nonergodic composition of two Mess3 components should give two fractal-filled cones, each telescopically scaling with the weight on its component.</p>
<p>The figure below shows the ground truth belief geometry, which serves as a nontrivial falsifiable prediction for what we should find in the transformer activations. The full beliefs live in 5 dimensions, and what is shown below are two 3D projections from the 5-simplex to the belief entries associated with each component. The still is taken at context position ; in <a href="https://belief-updates.pub/nonergodic-geometry/">the interactive version</a> an  slider moves through the context.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789058474/lexical_client_uploads/fkdsq5tq1mcwjlr71ggv.png" /></p>
<p><em><a href="https://belief-updates.pub/nonergodic-geometry/">Interactive version on the original post</a>.</em></p>
<p>We trained transformers on a nonergodic composition of two Mess3 generators. Our theory predicts that the activations should track the belief states. Because we have ground-truth access to the generator, we know the exact belief vector associated with each context position. A linear map fit from the residual stream to these ground-truth belief states recovers them on held-out contexts with R² ≈ 0.985 (compare to an untrained network, which is at ≈ 0.45), and the predicted geometry appears in the residual stream over training:</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788993515/lexical_client_uploads/t2beoumetexzi1pjhshk.gif" /></p>
<p><em><strong>The telescoping geometry emerging in early training</strong>, with the training loss shown beneath the cones. These are two 3-d projections of a 5-d geometry so each point appears in both cones. The points that are the face of one cone, appear as the low variance tip of the other cone.</em></p>
<p>We see our two telescoping cones, one for each component, scaling with the posterior weight on that component. This emerges as a direct consequence of pretraining on next-token cross-entropy alone. Nothing in the training objective tells the model directly about components, belief vectors, or simplices. The color here encodes the entropy that a Bayesian observer would have over which of the two components is active, given the context that led to that activation. The middle yellow region corresponds to contexts that are well explained by either component, and the states of maximum certainty are the darker tips and faces of the cones.</p>
<p>Below, we show the geometry of the converged model’s activations. The left panel is the cumulative variance explained by PCA of the final layer activations, drawn separately for contexts generated by each component ( and ); the two right panels show those same activations passed through the learned linear map to the predicted geometry. In <a href="https://belief-updates.pub/nonergodic-geometry/">the interactive version</a> you can filter activations by ground-truth posterior entropy or by context position. Dragging the maximum posterior entropy slider down toward 0 keeps only contexts where the evidence supports committing largely to one component. The CEV curves then climb faster, meaning the activations effectively fill <em>fewer dimensions</em> as the model hones in on a single component. Meanwhile, in the scatter plot, the cone for the now-unlikely component collapses toward the origin. Filtering to late sequence positions tells a similar, but noisier story. Additional context is increasingly likely to support just one component or the other, but it is also possible to observe long sequences that have similar likelihood under either component, or are even flat out misleading (the coin game on <a href="https://belief-updates.pub/nonergodic-geometry/">the original post</a> produces some of these).</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788994678/lexical_client_uploads/uqy96hodwsinixxhrk3b.png" /></p>
<p><em><a href="https://belief-updates.pub/nonergodic-geometry/">Interactive version on the original post</a>.</em></p>
<h2>Did it <em>have</em> to be this way?</h2>
<p>The way we derived the nonergodic belief geometry, it may seem almost as if there was no alternative for what the neural network should represent <span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-10">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-10">[10]</a></sup>
    </span>. In light of this, it is worth explicitly pointing out that t<strong>he predicted geometry is not something that just</strong> <em><strong>has</strong></em> <strong>to be present for the model to output correct next-token probabilities.</strong> <strong>This representation manifestly carries more distinctions between contexts than are implied by their differences in next-token prediction.</strong> While the beliefs live in 5 dimensions (a distribution over 6 hidden states), the next-token distribution lives only in 2 dimensions (a distribution over 3 possible tokens). Of course, this geometry does perfectly contain the distribution over the next token– but it also represents distinctions in the token after, the 10th token, and the joint probability distribution of the 3rd and 11th tokens conditioned on the 9th. It fully contains all distinctions that can be made between distributions over the future, yet it emerged only by looking one token ahead.</p>
<p>See the plot below, which shows the first three principal components of the 5-dimensional predictive geometry (right), colored by the associated next-token distribution. A small region in the next-token simplex (left) can correspond to significantly different parts of the full-future predictive geometry.</p>
<p>Scrolling around the next-token simplex on <a href="https://belief-updates.pub/nonergodic-geometry/">the original post</a> shows that some regions in the next token simplex correspond to unambiguous distributions over the future and other next-token distributions permit many different distributions over the full future.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788994679/lexical_client_uploads/eqwoidptisomutvcdlly.png" /></p>
<p><em><a href="https://belief-updates.pub/nonergodic-geometry/">Interactive version on the original post</a>.</em></p>
<p>We note that these canonical low-dimensional representations emerge most cleanly when we initialize network weights to be small, perhaps placing the network in the “rich” feature learning training regime studied in deep learning theory as opposed to the “lazy” one [9, 10].</p>
<h2>Parting thoughts</h2>
<p>The next token prediction task over nonergodic data requires two levels of inference: figuring out which generator is currently active, while also tracking what state that generator is in. One geometric implication for the activations of neural networks is a per-component <strong>projective embedding</strong> of the belief geometry with each component’s scale being the posterior weight accorded that component. With this geometry as a falsifiable prediction, we trained transformers on nonergodic compositions, and found this geometry linearly embedded in the residual stream.</p>
<p>Real data is made of many more sources than two, and they will overlap in their structure to different, and quite complicated, degrees. Some components will share most of their structure and differ in a few probabilities; others will share almost nothing; many will sit somewhere in between<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-11">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-11">[11]</a></sup>
    </span>. Taken together, we should expect a rich, hierarchical inference process to emerge from that: weights over components, weights over groups of components that look alike, and within each, the component’s own belief updating. This is one way to see why pretraining on such data produces in-context learning [7].</p>
<p>In closing, let’s revisit the humble coin. Some data is closer to a bag holding infinitely many coins: you draw a bias  from the continuum and start flipping (the problem Laplace solved in 1774); the sum over coins in the Bayesian updating equation becomes an integral, the finite set of weights (one for each component) become a continuum of weights, and the telescoping picture would need infinitely many cones. Yet, the formula for the weights would still only ever consults two numbers, the counts  and , so the distinct beliefs an observer can hold about the future still form a finite-dimensional predictive geometry, described by two parameters: an estimate (the fraction of heads) and how certain it is (the total number of flips). Whether a model stores such beliefs or computes them from running tallies, and what that means for a continuum of memoryfull components with nontrivial internal structure, and for generalization, is the subject of a post to come.</p>
<p>Our story supports a refinement to the picture of transformer representations as sums of <strong>sparse one-dimensional features</strong> that motivates sparse autoencoders [12, 13, 14]. For data with nonergodic structure, the right ansatz seems instead to be <strong>sparse dense subspaces</strong> — multi-dimensional geometries that correspond to inference-time Bayesian updating over an underlying world model that includes mutually exclusive<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-12">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-12">[12]</a></sup>
    </span> parts. The model uses many dimensions while a given component is in play, but eventually only a few components carry weight at any given time. Sparsity at the component level, density within each component. The same machinery extends naturally to compositions with internal factorization (each component itself a product of more elementary parts), and it predicts that models trained on factorizable data should discover those parts, represent them in correspondingly factored subspaces [15], and also simultaneously keep track of the meta dynamic over the components.</p>
<p>This picture, of transformer representations as a sparse sum of points within multidimensional subspaces of activation space, is consistent with recent work extending the “linear representation hypothesis” [12] to accommodate observations of multidimensional features in language models [16, 17]. These works suggest that neural network activations be modeled as sums of multidimensional features, whose value is represented as a point in subspaces of dimension greater than 1, but where most such features don’t have a defined value (or have value ~0) on most activations (they are sparse). We find this picture emerges naturally <em>from theory</em> as a consequence of performing prediction over a process consisting of nonergodic components.</p>
<hr />
