<h3>The Mess3 process</h3>
<p>The Mess3 process [5, 18] has three hidden states , and three observable tokens .</p>
<p>The process is defined by two parameters,  and , with dependent quantities  and . The two components used throughout this post are drawn from this family: the first uses  and the second uses .</p>
<p>The labeled transition matrices are:</p>
<p></p>
<h3>Training details</h3>
<p><strong>Data.</strong> Sequences are drawn from the nonergodic composition of the two Mess3 components defined above, mixed with equal weight. Each training sequence begins with a BOS token and then stays inside a single component for all 127 subsequent tokens; the two components share the same three-token alphabet, so no individual token reveals which component is active.</p>
<p><strong>Model.</strong> A four-layer decoder-only transformer (TransformerLens HookedTransformer): , four attention heads of dimension 32, gated GELU MLPs of width 512, RMS normalization, rotary position embeddings, context length 128, and a vocabulary of four tokens (three emissions plus BOS). Weights are initialized from a Gaussian with standard deviation 0.02, about  smaller than the TransformerLens default of .</p>
<p><strong>Optimization.</strong> AdamW (, no weight decay) at a constant learning rate of , with batches of 512 sequences.</p>
<p><strong>Metrics over Training.</strong> By step 10,000 the model’s next-token distribution sits within a few  nats per token of the optimal loss, and at the step-45,000 checkpoint used for the figures the gap is about . While the loss is falling, the regression error tracks it:  drops roughly as the excess loss to the  power in every layer past the first (every layer in which we find a belief representation). Once the loss reaches its floor, each layer settles onto a floor of its own, later for deeper layers. The animations in this post and the activation explorer use the residual stream after the third of the four blocks, where a linear map recovers the weighted belief vectors with held-out ; at the output of the final transformer block, it reaches .</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788994680/lexical_client_uploads/m15njxmo61wingicaqzl.png" /></p>
<h3>Generators of Data and the Geometry of Beliefs</h3>
<p>The three subsections below restate (at two levels of formality, presented one after the other) the mathematical machinery of our work [5]: hidden Markov models as latent generators of token sequences, belief updating as the structure of prediction, and the geometry of those beliefs.</p>
<p>Fundamentally we are trying to capture the situation relevant to the task prediction over sequences of data, especially when the data is generated by processes that are hidden to the predictor. As such, whatever our formal notion of a generator is, it should have an internal latent space that is hidden from the predictor, a set of rules for how that latent space changes through time (or context position), and a set of rules for how changes in the latent space relate to the observations (or tokens) emitted.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Everything below also holds for <em>generalized</em> HMMs (GHMMs), in which the transition operators may carry negative entries and the predictive vector need not be a probability distribution. Finite GHMMs represent a strictly wider class of processes than finite HMMs — the non-classical geometries of our companion post — but every example here is an ordinary HMM. In the following sections, we present this work at two levels of formality, marked <strong>General theory</strong> and <strong>Worked example</strong>. If you are interested in the concepts without necessarily the formal mathematics, we suggest skipping to the <em>Worked example</em> passages. If, instead, equations are what spark joy in you, the <em>General theory</em> passages are for you.</p>
</blockquote>
<h4>HMMs and their transition operators</h4>
<p><strong>General theory.</strong> HMMs are an extremely flexible model class: with enough hidden states, essentially any distribution over token sequences can be represented by one. An HMM is defined by the tuple</p>
<p></p>
<p>where  is the token alphabet,  is the latent space,  is an initial state vector, and each  is the operator describing latent dynamics for emission . The net transition operator  must have a right eigenvector  with unit eigenvalue. We can then interpret the dynamical systems latent space as carrying a conserved probability mass for which  is the integrator. This means we can interpret the probability of any token sequence  as being expressed by</p>
<p></p>
<p>It is in this sense that we say that an HMM generates a stochastic process.</p>
<p><strong>Worked example.</strong> Consider token sequences of the form <code>0</code>, then <code>1</code>, then a random bit, and repeats:<code>...0 1 R 0 1 R …</code>. Importantly, sequences can start at any of the three phases. We will call this the <em>Z1R process</em> (for “zero one random”). Here, we are interested in a <em>latent generator</em> of such data. As discussed above, it should have latent states, and dynamical rules telling us how those latent states change through time, and how those changes relate to token emissions.</p>
<p>One such generator for this particular data is a <em>hidden Markov model (HMM)</em>. It has three latent states:  , , and , which can be represented by circles in a graph as shown below.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788994681/lexical_client_uploads/do8edix5gpbf7gvsmuac.png" /></p>
<p>Sequences of tokens are generated by starting in a particular state (or a distribution over states), then following the arrows according to the probabilities on them. Upon choosing an arrow, the system moves to another latent state (which could be the same one), and emits a token, .</p>
<p>One can represent this system algebraically as well, as a set of token-labeled transition matrices, with one matrix, , per token. The entries of these matrices, , are the probability that the system, sitting in state , takes the arrow to state  and emits the token . In the figure above on the right, you can see the transition matrices for an HMM that generates the Z1R process.</p>
<p>The only other part needed to define an HMM is the initial state, denoted . In general this can be any probability distribution over the latent states of the system. When the HMM is generating a sequence, you can think of its starting state as being sampled from this initial state<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-13">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-13">[13]</a></sup>
    </span>.</p>
<h4>Prediction Over Data Generated by HMMs</h4>
<p>In the previous section we discussed <em>generators</em> of token sequence data. What is the computational structure of the prediction task, relative to the structure of the latent generator of the token sequence data?</p>
<p>Here we review the answer we established in our previous work: the information that a predictor must represent in order to take in sequences of token and predict future token sequences is given by <em>beliefs</em>, , over the hidden states of the latent generator of that data.</p>
<p><strong>General theory.</strong> We are interested here in the task of prediction of future token sequences given observations of past token sequences. Formally, the conditional probability of any future sequence given the observed context is</p>
<p></p>
<p>We call the vector encoding the past information the <strong>predictive vector</strong> (for an HMM, where it is a probability distribution over the hidden states, this is the <strong>belief state</strong> ):</p>
<p></p>
<p>This vector is the general answer to the question the coins raised. For the memoryless coins it collapses to the head/tail counts; in general it is everything the past tells you about the future, and nothing more.</p>
<p>Token by token, the same vector updates by one matrix multiplication and a renormalization,</p>
<p></p>
<p>where the denominator  is the probability the observer assigned to the token that just arrived — its next-token prediction.</p>
<p>Because the conditional probability above can be written as</p>
<p></p>
<p>iterating this update rule from  recovers exactly this closed form.</p>
<p><strong>Worked example.</strong> Intuitively, if we see a sequence of tokens from Z1R in context, like <code>0110</code>, we would do well to figure out which of the latent states the generator is in. Once we have that, we can then make a prediction for what the next token will be.</p>
<p>In general, given a particular sequence of tokens you will not be able to figure out exactly which latent state the HMM that generated that sequence is in. For instance upon seeing a <code>0</code> in context, which can be generated by taking arrows from either  to  or  to , we won’t know if the HMM is in state  or . But we can have an optimal belief about which state the HMM is in, in the form of a probability distribution over those states.</p>
<p><strong>An example of belief updating by hand</strong></p>
<p>Let’s do this by hand on Z1R. Before any tokens arrive, our belief about what state the HMM is uniform, . After seeing a <code>1</code>, each entry of the belief is multiplied by the chance that its state emits a <code>1</code>, the mass moves along that state’s arrow according to the transition matrix for the token <code>1</code>, , and the result is renormalized:</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788994681/lexical_client_uploads/mvexitizhhkxqqxr54bt.png" /></p>
<p>That <code>1</code> came either from  (which emits <code>1</code> with certainty, moving the process to ) or from  (which emits <code>1</code> only half the time, moving to ); weighing the two likelihoods leaves belief  on ,  on , and none on . Observe a second <code>1</code>:</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788994682/lexical_client_uploads/fatho9wsbnkmnxmzpxsz.png" /></p>
<p>Had the process been in , the next token would have been a <code>0</code> — the second <code>1</code> rules it out. The observer now knows the hidden state exactly: belief has <strong>synchronized</strong>, and it stays synchronized forever after, hopping deterministically around the corners of the simplex as the cycle turns.</p>
<p>Mathematically, belief updating is Bayes’ rule, with the HMM’s transition matrices as the likelihood. To update your belief upon seeing a new token, multiply your current belief by that token’s transition matrix and renormalize:</p>
<p></p>
<ul>
<li> — current belief (prior)</li>
<li> — operator for the token seen (likelihood)</li>
<li> — normalization (prob. of that token)</li>
<li> — updated belief (posterior)</li>
</ul>
<p>This update rule gives us a belief updating dynamic. The predictor has some current belief about the latent state of the generator, it sees a new token, and it dynamically updates its belief in the service of future token prediction.</p>
<h4>The geometry of beliefs</h4>
<p>Beliefs are vectors, so they have a geometry.</p>
<p><strong>General theory.</strong> Two contexts with identical predictive vectors make identical predictions about all future tokens; contexts with <em>similar</em> predictive vectors  make similar predictions because the probability for any future word differs by an amount proportional to . The collection of predictive vectors over all possible contexts forms a geometric arrangement in the latent space, determined entirely by the data-generating process. For a -dimensional latent space this arrangement lives in  dimensions (since predictive vectors are normalized).</p>
<p><strong>Worked example.</strong> In fact, for Z1R, from the stationary start only <em>seven</em> belief states are ever reachable: the center; three partially-resolved points —  after <code>0</code>,  after <code>1</code>,  after <code>10</code> — and the three corners. Every context, of any length, lands on one of these seven. This finite constellation in the 2-simplex <em>is</em> the belief state geometry of Z1R. Simple processes give finite constellations; richer processes (like the Mess3 process) fill their simplex with fractal ones; the machinery is identical either way.</p>

</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-4"><p>Note that the question “what is the next result of the coin flip given what we’ve seen so far?” is very related. The actual prediction for the next heads or tails (read: token) is given by a weighted vote of the coins:  <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-5"><p>For a full treatment, and another worked example — see the section “Generators of Data and the Geometry of Beliefs” (in the appendix). <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-5">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-6"><p>For the purposes of this post, you can think of a stochastic process as a set of sequences of tokens, and a probability distribution over those sequences. It is not a coincidence that this sounds like a training dataset for an LLM. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-6">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-7"><p>Because the composition can never leave the block it starts in, its sequence distribution is a mixture of the components’ distributions. To see this, write  for the probability that an HMM  assigns to a token sequence . We start from , and multiply by  in turn, and sum the entries (the section “Generators of Data and the Geometry of Beliefs” (in the appendix)). Because the composition can never leave the block it starts in, its sequence distribution is a mixture of the components’ distributions,  where  is the prior probability that component  is the one selected. If you are familiar with some ergodic theory: every stationary process is a mixture of ergodic ones, its ergodic decomposition. “Nonergodic” means that mixture has more than one term, and the block-diagonal construction is just that decomposition written as a single HMM, with the  as the mixing weights. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-7">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-8"><p>This is called the <em>direct sum</em> of the two components’ matrices, written . The two blocks sit on the diagonal and every entry off the diagonal is zero. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-8">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-9"><p>Define . Then , and dividing through gives  and . The components only interact through the denominator; and the interaction is entirely contained in the coefficient . <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-9">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-10"><p>There are, in fact, <em>many</em> alternative representations one can think of — ours comes from two assumptions: linear (up to normalization) representation updates and linear (no caveat) observation probability readout. Relax these assumptions just a bit, and we could encode the same information in the same number of dimensions: one normalized geometry per component, and a separate representation for  normalized to live on a “which component” simplex. This would yield a geometry where the conical projection is not natural. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-10">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-11"><p>We think that the nature of how different sources in the training data relate to each other in this way (that is, with respect to their predictive structures), and the consequences of that for model internals and behavior, is an incredibly important open question in interpretability. A closely related question is posed in terms of <em>task structure</em> in [11]: which of a model’s tasks share computation. Overlap in predictive structure is one candidate for what the distance between two tasks means, and for what gets shared, though it is not yet obvious what exactly “overlap” should mean beyond the simplest cases. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-11">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-12"><p>But perhaps related in certain ways! Also a topic of a post to come. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-12">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-13"><p>A natural choice is the <em>stationary distribution,</em> which in this case would be the uniform distribution over the three latent states: . <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-o8FmYXH6TZzJBfZTZ-13">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JfJ4WTRHmooPBWRFv/the-geometry-of-nonergodic-composition#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [An operationalization of opaque serial depth](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/x8BvtWxtoajBGHS3g/an-operationalization-of-opaque-serial-depth)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Currently, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2507.11473"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">chain-of-thought (CoT) is a valuable tool for overseeing AI models.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> However, some </span><a href="https://cdn.prod.website-files.com/663bd486c5e4c81588db7a1d/6a0ed93f9b4a6a65994235d8_Loss_of_Oversight%20(7).pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">architectural shifts could significantly reduce CoT monitorability</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We have </span><a href="https://www.redwoodresearch.org/blog/proposal-for-tracking-architecture-on-monitorability"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">recently proposed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that AI companies should transparently share ​​information about the degree to which their architectures may allow for latent reasoning and communication. To assist with this proposal, this document operationalizes a measure that serves as a proxy for the amount of unverbalized serial cognition a model can perform. Our measure is a specific instantiation of the notion of “opaque serial depth”, originally defined in a recent paper from GDM (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09786"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Brown-Cohen et al, 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To measure the opaque serial depth of a computation, Brown-Cohen et al. propose measuring the longest path in the computational graph which doesn’t pass through some form of “interpretable bottleneck”. Centrally, if one considers CoT tokens as “interpretable” but transformer hidden states as “non-interpretable”, then </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the opaque serial depth of a standard transformer is proportional to the number of layers</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref20d9rp7mifs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn20d9rp7mifs">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our main contribution in this document is a particular standard for what counts as an “interpretable bottleneck”. Roughly speaking, we want to consider nodes to be “interpretable bottlenecks” if they output text (as opposed to latent states), and were initialized from a pre-training prior. To make this notion more precise, we define the concept of </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">natural-language-rooted nodes (NL-rooted nodes)</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in a computation graph.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefiaxbcnous6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fniaxbcnous6">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> In order for a set of nodes in a model’s computational graph to count as NL-rooted, we require:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Natural-language initialization:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The model has been initialized from a pre-training prior to produce “natural-language” outputs at those nodes. (We have a very permissive notion of natural-language, which includes objects like “code”, “jsons” and “image patches”, and allows for distillation from models with garbled CoTs.)</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No expansion of the output space:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The only outputs of those nodes are tokens whose meanings were directly initialized from that pre-training prior. (Ruling out, for example, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22709"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">abstract CoT</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06769"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">COCONUT</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08888"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">full-bandwidth transformers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15778"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">token superpositions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.)</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No modifying the model to interpret the tokens as a different type of data:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The model should not be directly modified to use these tokens in ways radically disconnected from the pre-training prior. (This clause should not preclude natural training methods. It is primarily intended to rule out technical loopholes in the above bullet point, such as turning a </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08888"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">latent reasoning model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> into a model that superficially reasons in natural language via encoding and decoding its activations as natural language tokens.)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref88p7mz6dw4p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn88p7mz6dw4p">[3]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We elaborate on these criteria </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Definition_of_natural_language_rooted_nodes"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">below</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Importantly, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">all normal forms of post-training can be used without preventing nodes from being NL-rooted,</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> including prompting, distillation, and RL. (Even with aggressive length penalties or CoT supervision, and even if these forms of post-training make the CoT illegible in practice.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given this definition of NL-rooted nodes, we call our corresponding notion of opaque serial depth </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Natural-Language-rooted node”-Separated depth (NLS depth).</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Beyond defining this term, we also argue that it </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#NLS_depth_tracks_concerning_architecture_changes"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tracks concerning architectural changes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, discuss how to apply NLS depth to a number of example architectures (in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_A__Applying_NLS_depth_to_plausible_architectures"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix A</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), and discuss some alternative operationalizations of opaque serial depth (in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_B__Sensible_alternative_operationalizations"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix B</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tracking the NLS depth of AI systems is not a replacement for tracking monitorability or no-CoT capabilities; it is an important complement. NLS depth has some advantages not shared by these other measurements. In particular:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NLS depth is a relatively crisp and easy-to-quantify notion,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7wefm85xxpq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7wefm85xxpq">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> whereas it’s unclear how to quantify or measure monitorability (e.g., what tasks should this be measured on, what elicitation strategies should be used).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NLS depth can be adjudicated before training begins, just given the architecture, training recipe, and intended deployment-time configuration of an AI system. This makes it easier to use in decisions about what models to train.</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To achieve these properties, NLS depth is insensitive to some post-training choices that could in fact lead models to have significantly more or less interpretable CoT. However, we think NLS depth is a good (albeit imperfect) proxy</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3w7k62d5lg1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3w7k62d5lg1">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for the amount of opaque serial cognition that models can perform, which we think is highly </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_C__Systems_with_high_opaque_serial_reasoning_capabilities_are_likely_less_monitorable"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">relevant for monitorability</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. NLS depth </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#NLS_depth_tracks_concerning_architecture_changes"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">does a good job</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of separating architectures in the current natural language CoT paradigm (even with heavy scaling) from especially concerning architectures like scaled-up versions of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.08888"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">full-bandwidth transformers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (which can recur on opaque state throughout a full context window). Overall, we think it would be highly valuable to track the NLS depth of AI systems.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Definition of natural-language-rooted nodes</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before defining NLS depth we must define what “</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">natural-language-rooted</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” (NL-rooted) nodes in a computational graph are—these will serve as the “interpretable bottlenecks” in our definition of opaque serial depth.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Brown-Cohen et al. suggest two different candidate directions for defining what counts as an interpretable information bottleneck. Our concept of NL-rooted nodes is similar to the second one (suggested in their </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.09786#page=17"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix A.2</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), and has a similar motivation:</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Intuitively, the key reason that LLM chains of thought are interpretable is because we train LLMs to predict natural language, providing the LLM with a "natural language prior". As a result, the model's semantics for the tokens in the chain of thought should mimic the semantics that humans would ascribe to those tokens, because that is what would be useful for predicting text that was generated by humans.</span></i></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Inspired by this basic argument, we propose two criteria that together suggest that an intermediate node should be considered interpretable:</span></i></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Natural language prior: The intermediate representation is directly optimized either (1) to imitate human-written text (e.g., via pre-training or supervised fine-tuning) or (2) to look good to humans (e.g., RLHF (Ziegler et al., 2019)).</span></i></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. Limited optimization towards other incentives: There are relatively weak training incentives other than towards the natural language prior (ideally none). Incentives that directly push against the natural language prior are worse than incentives that are orthogonal to it.</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We pick a specific, fairly permissive version of the second criterion. Our intention is for this measure to track the difference between the current CoT paradigm and especially concerning architecture changes, rather than trying to draw a harder-to-define line about what type and degree of RL in the current paradigm should flip reasoning from counting as uninterpretable to interpretable.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In order for a set of nodes to be counted as NL-rooted, we require:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Natural-language initialization.</span></b></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No expansion of the output space.</span></b></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No modifying the model to interpret the tokens as a different type of data.</span></b></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also stress a specific property that is implied by these but that’s important enough to state separately: </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tokens in an NL rooted node should not be treated as a continuous intermediate state that is backpropagated through</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We will now expand on each of these four properties.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Natural-language initialization:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The model should have been initialized from a pre-training prior to imitate human-understandable media, with the “NL-rooted” nodes initially representing samples from this prior.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Human-understandable media” includes not only text, but also code, json files, images or audio (although note that multimodal CoT is somewhat more fraught; see discussion </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#NLS_depth_for_non_general_purpose_models"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">below</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). This admittedly makes “natural-language-rooted” a slightly unfortunate term.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Human-understandable media” is also presumptively assumed to include all outputs from NL-rooted nodes themselves, to allow for e.g. distillation from other models.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffc37v6il2b9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfc37v6il2b9">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Note that this means that the “human-understandable media” could, in some cases, end up being difficult to understand, if model CoTs grow to be very illegible.) If transparency about NLS depth is practiced, we would suggest that AI companies flag whether and how they use high-NLS-depth models in their distillation procedures, since it’s possible that low-NLS-depth models could inherit some dangers when distilled from high-NLS-depth models.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefftjfx1ej1vg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnftjfx1ej1vg">[7]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No expansion of the output space:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The nodes only represent tokens from an output space in which virtually</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkadgbh3w0f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkadgbh3w0f">[8]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> all tokens have been initialized from the pre-training prior, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">or</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have individually determined human-understandable meanings, like &lt;EOS&gt;, &lt;UNK&gt;, &lt;think&gt;, &lt;PAD&gt;. This is intended to rule out techniques such as </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06769"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">COCONUT</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22709"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">abstract CoT</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08888"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">full-bandwidth transformers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15778"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">token superpositions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. All of these techniques would be implemented by expanding the output space.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefahs6cjziq6h"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnahs6cjziq6h">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No modifying the model to interpret the tokens as a different type of data: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Not only do we require that a “natural-language-rooted node” continue operating over the same output space, we specify that this output space still be interpreted roughly as natural language in the same format as the model used in pre-training (which would be “continue a sequence of text” for a normal transformer).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That is, a node doesn’t count as natural-language-rooted if the model architecture has been changed such that one should expect the node’s outputs to be interpreted as a completely different type of data than the human-understandable media it was trained to imitate. (Normal training methods like RL or prompt distillation might shift how the model uses its outputs, but aren’t included in this definition because they won’t predictably result in the tokens being used as a proxy encoding of some other underlying type of data.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5csj7hgxjr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5csj7hgxjr">[10]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We are concerned here with methods that directly change the architecture, or train directly to cause the tokens to become a proxy for some other type of data.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are some examples of cases this is meant to explicitly rule out:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Optimizing the outputs of an NL-rooted node to imitate (or otherwise very strongly optimize a model to encode information about) an AI model’s opaque states. (Which we can operationalize as states that aren’t NL-rooted.) This would include NLA (</span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/nla/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">natural language autoencoders</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) that have been strongly optimized to optimally encode information about activations.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Taking multiple token samples, adding together the embeddings, and feeding the combination back into the model. This would count as changing the type of data since the sum of multiple different token embeddings is a type of input that the model has never encountered in pre-training, and it has no clear analogue in natural language.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflea2qbjvou7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlea2qbjvou7">[11]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Accordingly, the output would not count as NL-rooted.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsspxw6bdzn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsspxw6bdzn">[12]</a></sup></span></li></ol><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No treating the token as continuous intermediate states that are backpropagated through to train model weights:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> For current CoT models, each forward pass ends with a non-differentiable sampling step. As a result, when doing RL or SFT, gradients are never passed from the input layer of one pass through to the output layer of the previous pass.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We require the same property (no gradients from later nodes are backpropagated through the node to update model weights in training) in order for something to be counted as an NL-rooted node. We do not specify exactly how models sample tokens, but do require that the sampling operation itself similarly be treated as non-differentiable (algorithms that compute the loss directly from the token probabilities, such as policy gradients, are fine).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This criterion is probably superfluous given the above criteria.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2vsoru2nyix"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2vsoru2nyix">[13]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> However, we still think this criterion is worth mentioning separately, since backpropagation through representations seems like an especially likely way that they could be made hard-to-monitor. The concern is that backpropagating through NL-rooted nodes would cause changes in the nodes’ representations that wouldn’t correspond to any human-understandable semantics. Much more so than with RL (or e.g., evolutionary algorithms), where the search primarily happens within a distribution descended from pre-training.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The same restriction would apply to other algorithms that aren’t quite backpropagation, but that also treat the nodes as continuous and search for small local changes to them that optimize their usefulness for the layer in front of them. Also, backpropagating through a node that’s </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">shortly</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> before an NL-rooted node in the computational graph may cause it to no longer be NL-rooted. This should rarely matter for practical NLS depth calculations, though could matter for distillation. See footnote for details.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftvuzfc7i61g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntvuzfc7i61g">[14]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For worked examples of how to adjudicate whether something counts as a natural-language-rooted node or not, see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_A__Applying_NLS_depth_to_plausible_architectures"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix A</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Definition of NLS depth</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We now build upon the definition of natural-language-rooted nodes to define the main measure of interest, “Natural-Language-rooted node”-Separated depth (NLS depth).</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NLS depth:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A computation has NLS depth at most D if the function it implements can be represented by an </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">admissible circuit </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in which every </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">opaque path</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> has length at most D.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Three key concepts invoked in this definition are:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Admissible circuit</span></i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A circuit with gates that are arbitrary functions from two 32-bit values to a single 32-bit value, consisting of at most 10^26 gates. (The choice of “32” bits in particular, and the choice of “10^26” gates in particular, could be replaced by other reasonable choices and mostly does not make a substantive difference to the definition.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuo1dtoti5ec"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuo1dtoti5ec">[15]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) Note that a neural network (or system of neural networks) can easily be converted into an admissible circuit.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6kgxhan3q9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6kgxhan3q9">[16]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Opaque path</span></i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Any path in a circuit (with edges directed from inputs towards outputs) which doesn’t involve any circuit node marked as an interpretable bottleneck (i.e. a natural-language-rooted node).</span></li><li value="3"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Can be represented by</span></i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The strictest reading would require the circuit to compute exactly the same function as the neural network. We relax this slightly: the circuit may differ in ways that don't materially change the model's outputs, such as reordering reductions, altering floating-point rounding, or removing quantization. It may not differ in ways that materially change behavior. For instance a shallower model that merely matches the original's benchmark performance (e.g., via distillation) does not count as a representation of it.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6ka5y8hwypo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6ka5y8hwypo">[17]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is very similar to </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.09786#page=4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the definition given by Brown-Cohen et al</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; the only difference is in our choice of how to define an </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">admissible circuit</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref08hrtek9esbs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn08hrtek9esbs">[18]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This definition can technically be applied to any computation, but in practice we want to use this definition to measure the NLS depth of </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI systems</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Intuitively, an “AI system” is some machine learning system that is deployed to solve a problem (e.g., a math problem). A central example of an AI system is an LLM run on a prompt for one context-length. Some other objects that count as AI systems include: an agent run with compaction, an agent that stores memories, an agent that performs many backpropagations over the course of an inference rollout (e.g., as in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.23675"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TTT</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), and a swarm of communicating agents. An example of a computation that we wouldn’t want to consider an AI system is “the entire process of training an AI model”. When an LLM interacts with humans or with a computer via tool calls, we also do not consider that human or computer to be part of the AI system.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here is some intuition about the NLS depth of some of the AI systems mentioned above:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For a model that reasons in natural-language CoT, the NLS depth of an entire inference rollout is the same as the NLS depth of a single forward pass (done at maximum context length). This is true even if the inference rollout involves compacting the context to a textual summary of the context, storing textual memories during the context, and even if the inference rollout involves communicating in text with other agents.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For a model that does latent reasoning (like Meta’s </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06769"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">COCONUT</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), the NLS depth of an inference rollout is the number of latent reasoning steps times the NLS depth of a single forward pass. Similarly, for a looped transformer, the NLS depth is the depth of a loop iteration times the number of loops. This is true in each case even if tokens are generated in parallel with the latent state, since there is still some long path through opaque state.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If a model stores and later reads opaque memories, then the longest chain of opaque memories would determine the NLS depth.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Similarly, if you had a swarm of agents that were communicating using opaque state, then the longest chain of opaque state would determine the NLS depth.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The NLS depth of a given AI system can vary across different deployment configurations and inputs. Current LLMs have slightly higher depth at longer context lengths (the scaling of depth with sequence length is generally quite slow).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvht13sfv2hn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvht13sfv2hn">[19]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> More importantly, some architectures could allow for </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.05171"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">variable length forward passes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We expect that the maximum NLS depth used in practice is the most important version to track (but the rough distribution of NLS depth as well as the maximum readily feasible and performant NLS depth could also be informative).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NLS depth is a fairly natural property of a model, and it’s fairly easy to compute a good bound on a model’s depth—we give an example of this computation for Gemma3-1B in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_D__Worked_example_of_bounding_depth"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix D</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In particular, given this document and the details of an architecture, existing consumer AI models are capable of computing bounds we believe to be reasonably close to optimal. The NLS depth of a standard transformer (with non-sparse attention) is roughly </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">number of layers · (4⌈log₂ hidden_dim⌉ + ⌈log₂ sequence_length⌉ + 17)</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzpjfjd34uo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzpjfjd34uo">[20]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpgrbrqyeac"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpgrbrqyeac">[21]</a></sup></span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NLS depth is a better notion to report than number of layers, because “layer” is an informal definition, and it may be hard to decide what counts as a “layer” for new architectures. Fortunately, circuit depth is a mathematically rigorous definition that captures essentially the same thing. Indeed, the intuitive number of layers and the circuit depth are strongly correlated in open-source models, as shown in Figure 1.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/x8BvtWxtoajBGHS3g/90b0f43b7cf2cd99d33fb83784247f59c1c38aa313bb90621de24fe2e14a807c/jmtidcy4qvmgmvt4i0rp" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 1: There’s a strong correlation between NLS depth and the number of layers in open-source models.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note: Models like Kimi-K3 that use Gated DeltaNets and DeepSeek-V4-Pro have significantly higher NLS depth per layer than the other more standard transformers listed in Figure 1. We think that the architectural changes that give Kimi-K3 higher depth is indeed somewhat concerning from a monitorability perspective. On the other hand, we consider DeepSeek-V4-Pro’s high depth to be due to a flaw in our definition, since it’s only large due to very serially-intensive but very low-FLOP computations. See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_G__Issues_with_low_FLOP_serially_intense_computations"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix G</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for more details on this.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_A__Applying_NLS_depth_to_plausible_architectures"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A we provide more intuition for NLS depth by going through examples of architecture changes that do or do not substantially increase NLS depth.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NLS depth tracks concerning architecture changes</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A core reason why NLS depth seems valuable to track is that models in the historical transformer paradigm would have substantially lower NLS depth than architectures we believe are especially concerning for monitorability, such as </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.08888"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">latent reasoning architectures</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which would be able to perform large amounts of computation opaquely.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even many orders of magnitude of scaling of historical transformer architectures won’t increase depth very much. For a historical trend of how fast depth has been scaling in open source models, see Figure 2. For more analysis of how fast we expect NLS depth to scale in the standard CoT paradigm, including historical scaling with active parameter count and analysis based on scaling laws, see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_E__NLS_depth_scaling_is_very_slow_for_the_classic_transformer_architecture"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix E</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/x8BvtWxtoajBGHS3g/c4618edfd5ee581d3f2ebe156ecb37236a11f6f58cb62e1c1dca1136c105979d/f7t6t1oyzdtou6t6axfd" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 2: Open source models have not seen significant depth scaling over recent years.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead of scaling opaque serial depth, AI developers have been increasing computational depth via </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">inference-time scaling</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, i.e., making use of more natural language reasoning. No open-source CoT model has NLS depth more than 17,000. Of course we don’t know the depths of closed-source models but would find it surprising if any of those had more than twice this NLS depth.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, if companies switched to </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.08888"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">latent reasoning architectures</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the NLS depth would quickly increase a large amount, as illustrated in Figure 3.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/e97e6742a725a12a13e5b730c24753700a471d3dc510b453be55a6495a84caa9/cmkzbzy6rxht9agize6a" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 3: Even some fairly small (hypothetical) latent reasoning architectures would have much larger NLS depth than the largest open-source CoT models. (Note the log scale.)</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An architectural change that would lead to a significant but less extreme increase in NLS depth is a looped transformer (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2502.05171"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Geiping et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). These would be less clearly separated from the current paradigm, since a looped transformer could have similar depth to normal CoT models (e.g. if a 120 layer model was replaced with two copies of 60 layers) but could also be scaled to be much deeper. For these models, we think it’s likely that NLS depth would be a decent proxy for the quantitative degree of concern (and even a 2x scaling in depth enabled by looping would have depth equivalent to years of scaling within the CoT paradigm).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note: OpenAI’s latest model Astra </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra/evaluations-with-challenging-prompts"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">has substantially lower CoT monitorability</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> than their previous models. The cause of this is unclear, but public evidence is consistent with architectural changes that result in higher depth being a significant contributing factor.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3ljy8ipuw4a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3ljy8ipuw4a">[22]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One concerning architectural change that NLS depth fails to track is a switch from deeply recurrent models communicating in natural language with each other, to deeply recurrent models communicating in opaque state. See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_F__Maximum_FLOP_of_an_opaque_system"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix F</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for a different proxy for opaque capabilities that captures this.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this document we’ve defined NLS depth—an operationalization of opaque serial depth. Intuitively, a model has low NLS depth if it is a low-depth transformer that operates on text rather than opaque states. We think that a switch to architectures with substantially higher NLS depth would likely lead to models with much higher opaque serial capabilities, which are harder to oversee. We think it’s valuable for AI companies to track and be transparent about the NLS depth of their models.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix A: Applying NLS depth to plausible architectures</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We now clarify our operationalization by discussing how NLS depth would change as a result of various changes to architecture or model training processes. Specifically, we’ll discuss some changes that would and wouldn’t substantially increase NLS depth.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Examples that don’t increase NLS depth</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CoT that naturally becomes garbled over training. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the CoT naturally becomes hard to understand over the course of training, that does not increase NLS depth, since the CoT would still count as NL-rooted. This is by design: we want our definition to be possible to adjudicate before training. Keeping CoTs legible and informative is important, but it is harder to operationalize and measure. We also suspect it will be more tractable to find technical solutions to RL resulting in CoT staying legible and faithful if models continue to have low NLS depth.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Training on CoT.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Reasonable training on the CoT doesn’t prevent it from being “NL-rooted” so it doesn’t increase an architecture’s NLS depth. For instance, a model’s NLS depth is not increased just because training penalizes CoTs that do “language switching”, or are otherwise garbled. Even though such training maintains low NLS depth, we recommend that AI companies proceed with caution when training on CoT (and consider not doing so), as this can have adverse effects on CoT monitorability. Egregious forms of training, such as intentionally causing the model to interpret its tokens in ways radically disconnected from the pre-training prior (e.g., by imitating the activations of an AI trained to reason with full opaque recurrence), would violate the “No modifying the model to interpret the tokens as a different type of data” requirement, thereby making the tokens not be NL-rooted.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Folding multiple steps of reasoning into a forward pass. </span></b><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2405.14838"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some work</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> aims to decrease the length of LLM reasoning by training the model to compute multiple steps of the chain of thought in a single forward pass. This has some similarity in ethos to COCONUT, so we list it here. Unlike COCONUT, this architectural choice does not increase NLS depth. This is because compressing reasoning in this way doesn’t increase the depth of the model’s forward pass, doesn’t introduce anything new to the output space, and doesn’t make the model treat the tokens as a different type of data.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Edit-based sequence generation. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One proposed alternative to autoregressive CoT is to allow models to make local </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">edits </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(i.e. insertions and deletions, as opposed to just appends) to their reasoning trace. Early experiments on such architectures include </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1905.11006"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Levenshtein</span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1905.11006"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Transformer</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1902.03249"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Insertion Transformer</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Each step of reasoning for such models is still a low-depth function taking natural language tokens to natural language tokens, and these tokens are treated by the model the same way that they’re treated during pre-training, so this architectural choice does not increase NLS depth.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Online training.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A naive reading of the definition would assign every model extremely large NLS depth, since training backpropagates through it: Backpropagating through a model conveys information from the output back to earlier layers in a form other than natural language. Clearly, this alone should not cause an architecture to be considered as having high NLS depth. We do not consider the backpropagation done on a model to be part of the AI system itself; rather, this is some outer optimization process the company is running on their model. A company could have every single inference generation involve a huge number of backprops, used effectively as a way to pass information between forward passes, but as long as nothing like that is happening, training (including online training in deployment) does not increase NLS depth.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI models modifying AI weights. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Backpropagation is one way that an AI’s output can end up opaquely modifying the model’s weights. As noted above, this does not increase NLS depth (so long as backpropagation is not used as part of the AI system’s inference-time reasoning, as we’ll discuss later). We would like to apply a similar standard to any other feedback loops whereby AIs modify their own weights (or the weights of other AIs). For instance, models will be increasingly used in the future to write the code used for training future models, for generating synthetic data to train on, or even directly modifying their own weights (e.g. by finding good steering vectors). We consider these kinds of operations fine, so long as there is still an NL-rooted bottleneck involved (e.g. the AI writes code which is then used to find a steering vector, as opposed to having the architecture just pipe some chunk of final-layer activations in to produce a steering vector). Only extremely egregious forms of this would increase NLS depth, such as training the model to update its own weights a large number of times over the course of each inference generation.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Discrete language diffusion with a token-only bottleneck.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> There are a number of different ways to do language diffusion, and some of them would lead to a substantially larger NLS depth while others wouldn’t. In diffusion models like </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2502.09992"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LLaDA</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15487"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dream</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, each denoising step takes as input and output an explicit sequence of tokens (and possibly some masked tokens), and nothing else. These tokens and the diffusion operation are initialized based on a human-understandable prior and not explicitly trained in a way that obviously results in losing this correspondence, so would count as NL-rooted. Frontier-scale discrete diffusion models with a token-only bottleneck would therefore not have high NLS depth compared to other frontier-scale models. However, continuous text diffusion models and latent diffusion models like </span><a href="https://deepmind.google/models/gemma/diffusiongemma/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">DiffusionGemma</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> do have high NLS depth, as discussed below.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(We suspect that text diffusion models will produce substantially less-legible reasoning than true autoregressive CoT. The reasoning happens in a much more global way, and the intermediate states are likely to look garbled in ways that may make it difficult to track what is happening. We expect this disparity would become even more accentuated if language diffusion models were deployed at scale and subject to large amounts of RL pressure. So, it might be reasonable to adopt an alternative definition that also classified discrete language diffusion as having high opaque serial depth. But NLS depth doesn’t do so.)</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Examples that moderately increase NLS depth</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mamba.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> At inference time, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.00752"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mamba</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> models are usually evaluated recurrently — that is, instead of just generating tokens, some information is passed through an opaque hidden state. However, there is a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mathematically equivalent</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> way of evaluating in parallel, where the next token can indeed be generated given just the previous tokens. This takes longer (there is a logarithmic depth overhead), but not </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">linearly</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> longer, as would be the case for a true recurrent model. As a result, the NLS depth of Mamba models is only moderately higher than a comparable transformer model, even if it is run in recurrent mode in practice.</span></p><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.06484"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">DeltaNets</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Just like with Mamba, DeltaNets are usually evaluated recurrently, but each token could instead be computed by a fairly low-depth computation with a polylogarithmic depth overhead.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmf1mx6p0o7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmf1mx6p0o7">[23]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So, DeltaNet architectures like Kimi-K3 have a moderate, but not massive depth increase compared to standard transformers.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Looped transformers. </span></b><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2502.05171"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Looped transformers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> repeat some of their forward pass several times in between producing tokens. If the entire forward pass is repeated k times between each token, this increases the NLS depth by a factor of k compared to one forward pass. Depending on k, this might be a moderate or very large increase in NLS depth.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Edited-to-add: Note that the same paper also proposes a design where the KV-cache would be shared between layers at different depths. This variant would very substantially increase NLS depth. Scaling would be linear with the number of tokens. For more, see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LaR7joZu4QyaM7hqq/recurrent-kv-cache-sharing-may-undermine-the-bounded-depth"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MBaert (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Examples that substantially increase NLS depth</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recurrence / soft tokens. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scaled-up versions of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2412.06769"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">COCONUT</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are a canonical example of an architecture with high opaque serial depth. Instead of reasoning in natural language tokens, COCONUT reasons by feeding its entire hidden state back into its first layer. The hidden states themselves are not NL-rooted, since they both make use of a larger output space </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are backpropagated through, and so each sequential step of reasoning increases NLS depth. If COCONUT performed 1000 steps of reasoning, its NLS depth would be 1000x larger than the depth of a single forward pass.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Abstract CoT. </span></b><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2604.22709"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some recent work</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> takes a CoT model, and trains it to make use of an expanded token set, without encouraging any particular relationship to human language. Unlike COCONUT, these are still discrete tokens — however, they make use of an expanded output space, and so their NLS depth is the depth of a forward pass times the number of steps of reasoning.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Superposition. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One approach is to express the full probability distribution over tokens by creating a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">concept token</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> represented by the probability-weighted mixture of token embeddings, and feeding this into the next model. (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15778"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Zhang et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14827"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Zhuang et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.03440"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Wu et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). This makes use of an expanded output space (either passing along the probability distribution itself or token embeddings that were never present during pre-training) and therefore the NLS depth would be the depth of a forward pass times the number of steps of reasoning. The same thing would be true even if you just sampled tokens and then averaged them together, since this usage would be to interpret the tokens as a different type of data than during pre-training. (Where token embeddings are not averaged together like that.)</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Opaque memory banks.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Currently, some AI models form text-based memories, retaining bits of context to inform future interactions. In the future, models might instead form </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">opaque</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> memories — for instance, storing particular activations and feeding them back as inputs for future generations. This opaque loop operates on a longer timescale than e.g., COCONUT, so is somewhat less concerning, but would still increase NLS depth (and does indeed seem concerning). If this was considered undesirable, we discuss an alternative definition that would allow some forms of opaque memory without increased opaque serial depth </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Allow_opaque_loops_over_long_timescales"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Continuous or latent-augmented language diffusion.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> While discrete diffusion with a token-only bottleneck doesn’t substantially increase depth according to our definition, most recent language diffusion models do not follow this form. Instead, they are either </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">continuous diffusion </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">models, as in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2305.18619"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plaid</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">latent-augmented diffusion</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> models, as in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.01329"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CADD</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://deepmind.google/models/gemma/diffusiongemma/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">DiffusionGemma</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In the former case, the intermediate states are </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">continuously-noised</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> versions of the text embeddings, as opposed to actual text. In the latter, the text diffusion process is accompanied by another parallel process over continuous latents. Both of these use states that have not been pre-trained to have a clear correspondence to human-understandable media, and so these reasoning steps would substantially increase NLS depth.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(GDM has a </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.20560"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">recent paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Engels et al., 2026) arguing that they can often understand what these latents are encoding via mechanistic interpretability. This sort of work is worthwhile, both in the case of diffusion language models and for interpreting hidden states in models more generally. However, we think it is unlikely that such white-box interpretability will be able to fully substitute for CoT, and predict it would become less effective with increased model scale.)</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Opaque models + NLAs. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We now describe an architecture designed to appear to have low NLS depth while still being hard to monitor for similar reasons as classic opaque recurrence architectures. As we’ll explain, the attempt is in fact classified as high NLS depth. Suppose an AI is produced as follows: Starting with a pretrained model, train an </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/nla/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NLA</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to convert from final-layer activations to large blocks of text. Compose this model with itself many times, by having each step’s final-layer activations encoded by the NLA, then decoded by the NLA, then passed into the next step. Apply outcome-based RL on this new model.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One could make an argument that the intermediate steps here are NL-rooted. Indeed, the composition of “NLA decoding, step, NLA encoding” is a low-depth circuit that takes in natural language and outputs natural language. And one could try to argue that NLAs are initialized by making reasonable-sounding guesses about what the model might be thinking, and that this corresponds to an initialization in a human-understandable prior. However, if the NLA outputs large enough blocks of text to get (near) perfect reconstruction loss, this system is functionally equivalent to opaque recurrence! One could even imagine saying that, as a “performance optimization”, the NLA encoding / decoding steps will be removed in practice. So it seems important to spell out why the intermediate states would not count as NL-rooted.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The primary objection is: this structure seems to be training the models to interpret the tokens as a different type of data. The tokens are being directly optimized to be useful for predicting opaque state of models (via reconstruction loss), which means that they will very plausibly just become an arbitrary representation of this data rather than representing the semantics from their initial training.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Test-Time Training (TTT). </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some model architectures perform backpropagation many times during the course of a single inference rollout. By default, these backpropagation steps count as contributing to the NLS depth of the computation — backpropagation is a way of storing opaque state in the weights between steps. This is in contrast to online training that happens in between inference rollouts (i.e. a separate outer loop not part of the inference process), which doesn’t increase the NLS depth of the model (this is because online training seems like a pretty separate process from the model’s reasoning, so we don’t count it as part of any “AI system” that you should track the NLS depth of).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are reasonably happy with this classification, although we think that there is room for debate here. Here’s why we think it’s worth counting TTT as substantially increasing NLS depth:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some ways of modifying the weights during inference are clearly pretty concerning: for instance, if you always wrote the last layer activations down in a place that can be accessed during the first layer of computation when computing the following token, this allows for arbitrarily deep opaque recurrence.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In principle, it should be possible to write a configuration of model weights such that backpropagation really does convey all of the information about the last layer activations back to the first layer in a readable form.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems hard a priori to rule out that a particular TTT scheme will end up effectively doing something like this.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That said, TTT schemes generally seem much less concerning to us than, e.g., COCONUT, and the mechanism of recurrence is reasonably different. We therefore think that reasonable alternative definitions of opaque serial depth could treat many TTT schemes differently. (Though see the “Meta-learned Test-Time Training” section below for discussion of schemes we find more concerning.)
