However, increasing the bit rate would have some issues that would require some modifications to other parts of the definition. At the very least, parameters would have to be taken as inputs rather than be wired into the circuit, so that entire learned components of the neural network with a small number of outputs and inputs couldn’t be fully compressed into a single step. (This could probably be specified by requiring a model’s computational graph to be identical throughout training.) With these corresponding adjustments, a change in bit-rate would not be entirely unprincipled as a method for handling this kind of problem. In particular, it would allow for reducing serial depth most drastically when a serially deep algorithm has very few inputs and learned parameters, which also means that it wouldn’t be able to substantially contribute to serially opaque cognition. However, it wouldn’t be a complete fix. For example, a Sinkhorn-Knopp computation that normalized a very large table would require a yet-larger bit-rate, and it wouldn’t be feasible to set a bit-rate so high that it addresses all problems like this.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/x8BvtWxtoajBGHS3g/an-operationalization-of-opaque-serial-depth#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Proposal for tracking the effects of architecture on monitorability](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hLPGv8QjPcNLtDp3A/proposal-for-tracking-the-effects-of-architecture-on)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Architectures that incorporate opaque recurrence or allow for agents to communicate with each other using latents could rapidly make it much harder to monitor chains of thought or communication (we’ll refer to this property as “monitorability” going forward).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5zkru1nwdow"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5zkru1nwdow">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> As companies begin to explore such architectures, we believe it is important to transparently share evidence about how monitorability varies with architecture and training method. To inform the scientific debate on how to make tradeoffs between performance and monitorability, we believe AI companies should:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Regularly report externally verified information about the degree to which their architectures may allow for latent reasoning or communication. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Companies should publicly disclose enough information about architectures to allow external scientists to determine whether they could potentially enable models to perform much more complex reasoning without this reasoning appearing in the chain of thought (“latent reasoning”) or allow for latent communication between different instances of a model.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Following </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09786"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, we propose measuring </span><a href="https://www.redwoodresearch.org/blog/proposal-for-tracking-architecture-on-monitorability" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">opaque serial depth</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as a minimally-invasive proxy for the degree to which an architecture may enable latent reasoning, though companies could provide sufficient architecture transparency in other ways. We propose that companies work with third-party evaluators to produce independently verified reports of the rough distribution of opaque serial depth across all their </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">near-frontier </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">models.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefac4hp6wt08u"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnac4hp6wt08u">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> External verification wouldn’t require third parties seeing the architecture of these models; interviews with employees along with a process for employees to whistleblow to the third party could be sufficient.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">near-frontier </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">models, we mean all general-purpose</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref33f7k6xq5ed"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn33f7k6xq5ed">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> internal models at least as capable as the best public models from six months ago.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqktw7b9dyms"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqktw7b9dyms">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Because it is important to understand the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">relationship </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">between monitorability and architecture — and not just the monitorability of deployed models — the models covered in this survey should include purely internal R&amp;D prototypes if they have near-frontier capabilities.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Opaque serial depth can also serve as an incomplete proxy for latent communication</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvtlyfee7ubp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvtlyfee7ubp">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but companies should ideally share additional high-level information about the architecture and structure of communication between agents (e.g., whether AIs communicate in latents, whether shared ‘memories’ are stored in latents, whether agents receive parts of the KV cache of other agents).</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The relationship between well-elicited</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpurmidz8kok"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpurmidz8kok">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> no-CoT capabilities and overall capabilities could serve as an additional or alternative proxy measurement. More concretely, companies could build a dataset to measure no-CoT capabilities, project how much some change to architecture or training methods </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eRmzz8J8Qkzqvzrgg/astra-can-do-a-concerning-amount-with-no-chain-of-thought"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">increases no-CoT ECI relative to how much that change increases overall ECI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefx6b83wch51"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnx6b83wch51">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and work with third-parties to produce independently verified reports of the extent to which new methods might result in a disproportionately large increase in no-CoT ECI.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcmm4bshm3iu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncmm4bshm3iu">[8]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This reporting should have reasonable coverage over all near-frontier models.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Regularly share evidence about monitorability and have third parties review this. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Companies should publicly share their views about the monitorability of their near-frontier models and how this varies across architectures and training methods, along with any evidence or information they have about this.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqpar64bbvsl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqpar64bbvsl">[9]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Ideally, companies would run </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">monitorability stress tests</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to see if models can be elicited or fine-tuned to perform reasoning without this being reflected in their chains of thought, or communicate with other models in ways that are hard to monitor.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefre2vt7xhm58"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnre2vt7xhm58">[10]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  An initial sketch of what stress tests of CoT monitorability could look like is in an appendix below.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Companies should give third-party experts access to the unredacted results from these experiments, and third-party experts should:</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a. Assess whether these experiments and other evidence are accurately describe</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">b. Run their own experiments to gather more evidence about CoT monitorability and how this varies across training methods and architectures, and to gather evidence about any other important claims companies made.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">c. Report their findings to the public, with redactions for IP as appropriate.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As above, this should have reasonable coverage over models at least as capable as the best public models from six months ago, including internal R&amp;D prototypes if they are near the frontier. Evidence sharing and third-party reviews should occur regularly (e.g., every 6 months) and potentially whenever a new architecture or training method results in a near-frontier model with significantly increased opaque serial depth.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgpx5nhxdsgi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngpx5nhxdsgi">[11]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Publish a policy regarding architectures that could enable greater latent reasoning, and provide information to help assess compliance. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Companies should publish</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuve68qvm5id"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuve68qvm5id">[12]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a policy document that clearly explains:</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a. Their views on what kinds of architectures could enable much greater latent reasoning or otherwise degrade monitorability, and their operationalization of how they would identify such architectures. As above, we propose opaque serial depth as a baseline operationalization for latent reasoning. Ideally, companies would also provide criteria for architectural or training features that may result in opaque communication between agents.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">b. What kind of evidence they would require from monitorability stress tests (or other tests) to determine that architectures that meet the criteria for a) don’t in fact decrease monitorability.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">c. How they plan to make tradeoffs between performance and monitorability in their architecture development research.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To enable earlier scientific debate about the appropriate policies, companies should provide visibility into their degree of investment into architectures that meet the criteria for a).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4ruq42ezg3w"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4ruq42ezg3w">[13]</a></sup></span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While this reporting would involve sharing some intellectual property, we believe that it is strongly in the public interest to have visibility into how companies are making tradeoffs between performance and monitorability.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: A sketch of what stress tests of CoT monitorability could look like</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitorability tests can study monitorability both in control settings and on real misbehaviors from deployment. The next sections give a basic sketch of what some of these experiments could look like (focused on CoT and action monitorability rather than monitoring communication between agents).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In addition to assessing detection of specific problematic behaviors, ideally experiments would also measure how easily we can qualitatively understand and predict behavior.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Testing monitorability in control settings</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Control settings such as </span><a href="https://www.linuxarena.ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Linux Arena</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> provide “main tasks” along with “side tasks” which represent malicious behavior.  A red team elicits the model to covertly pursue the side task, while control measures try to catch the model or prevent the side task from being achieved.  This gives us a very natural way to measure one aspect of monitorability: a model is more monitorable to the extent that CoT monitoring makes it unable to achieve the side task without being caught.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It would be worthwhile to test several different levels of elicitation to establish a range of possibilities for how well the model might evade monitoring:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the lowest level of elicitation, experiments could give the model a very simple prompt like “try to do the side task without being caught by a CoT monitor”.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the next level of elicitation, experiments could involve optimizing the model’s prompt.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the next level of elicitation, experiments would involve fine-tuning the model to be better at covertly achieving the side task. Some amount of training would be needed to potentially be robust to the possibility that the model is sandbagging the evaluation.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It could also be worthwhile to study the effects of heavily training the model to be better at covertly achieving the side task (while avoiding overfitting to a particular model), but this may not be needed.</span></li></ol><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Testing monitorability on deployment-time misbehaviors</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In addition to experiments in control settings, researchers could assess monitorability on natural examples of model misbehavior.  This requires them to have access to examples of misbehavior found by the company or affordances to find such examples.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given natural examples of misbehavior, they could:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Check to what extent the model’s CoT spells out the model’s decision to do the misbehavior and the model’s planning of how to execute the misbehavior. They should especially pay attention to the largest steps of reasoning that didn’t get verbalized.  </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Check how obvious the misbehavior is to a weak trusted monitor reading the CoT.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fine-tune / elicit the model to execute some natural misbehaviors without revealing intent or plans in the CoT, and see if this teaches it to successfully execute other natural misbehaviors without being caught by CoT monitors.</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Testing qualitative monitorability on (hopefully realistic) model organisms</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These experiments would be significantly more involved than the above experiments, but could be worth running if resourcing is available.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To check whether some architecture or training method reduces how readily overseers can find detailed and accurate explanations of misaligned behaviors, we can run auditing games where auditors use access to chain-of-thought and potentially other affordances to diagnose diverse, realistic misbehaviors by model organisms (for which we have ground truth on the nature of their misbehaviors). Model organisms can also be elicited to obfuscate the nature of their misalignment. Examples of such auditing games include </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2602.22755"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AuditBench</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2512.07810"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Auditing games for sandbagging</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We consider a model organism to be realistic if it is at least as robust to auditing as current production models, and if it behaves in ways we would expect to be caused by the most plausible mechanisms by which current/future models become misaligned (e.g. training on reward-hackable environments), whether or not it is in fact produced through these mechanisms. It may be difficult to achieve this level of realism while meeting other constraints (e.g., having a known correct explanation).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We consider a detailed, qualitative explanation to consist of the following,:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What the misaligned behavior is: a relatively complete and general description of what unwanted actions the model tends to take</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What motives explain the misaligned behaviors: what outcomes these actions optimize for (if any), or other drives/heuristics that accurately predict the behaviors</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What contexts activate these misaligned behaviors: e.g. type of usage / task distribution, nature of oversight, specific triggers such as a passphrase or belief that the task is impossible</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What training conditions caused these misaligned behaviors: what kind of data and training methods might have produced the observed generalization</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For the last bullet, investigators might also access training rollouts (including CoT that occurs in these rollouts) etc. as would be realistic in production audits.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5zkru1nwdow"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5zkru1nwdow">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chain-of-thought monitors should look at actions the model is taking in addition to their reasoning. The interaction between CoT monitoring and action monitoring could be important (e.g., if agents were allowed to reason freely in their CoT or in latent reasoning they could carefully reason through how to take more surreptitious actions, and if actions weren’t monitored they could do the malign action in some very obvious way that doesn’t require much reasoning).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnac4hp6wt08u"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefac4hp6wt08u">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This methodology used to obtain this distribution should be published. The published depth distribution information would ideally include the largest approximate opaque serial depth among near-frontier models that see non-trivial usage and the approximate opaque serial depth for representative (as in, widely used internally) near-frontier models. The methodology the company is using to compute the depth should also be made public (with redactions for IP as needed) and used to compute and publish depths for open-weight models. (Any circuit transformations used to obtain a lower depth should also be applied to these open-weight models for fair comparison.) When reporting approximate depths, it could be best to report the depth as a multiple of the depth of some open-weight model (computed with the same methodology).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn33f7k6xq5ed"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref33f7k6xq5ed">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As in, AIs that can do a variety of tasks such as math, coding, and writing. This is intended to exclude specialized image generation models. An attachment to a general purpose AI which is solely for the purpose of image generation (and doesn’t increase general purpose capability outside of image generation) can be excluded.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqktw7b9dyms"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqktw7b9dyms">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s important to cover research prototypes that haven’t yet reached the frontier but are promising enough that they have been significantly scaled up. This is to ensure that public scientific understanding of monitorability can keep up with rapidly progressing research on new architectures. It is also cheaper for an AI company to stop further development of insufficiently-monitorable architectures early in the R&amp;D process.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvtlyfee7ubp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvtlyfee7ubp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In particular, companies could report the opaque serial depth of a larger computation which involves communication between multiple different AI models. One reason why this wouldn’t be a complete measure is that various approaches for sharing parts of the KV cache between agents could result in concerning latent communication while only yielding small increases in depth.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpurmidz8kok"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpurmidz8kok">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">E.g., after significant fine-tuning on an IID set of tasks. If </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uvhuZHFtrgk8kNiZc/astra-is-much-better-at-reasoning-with-filler-tokens-than" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">filler tokens improve performance</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, then the most performant number of filler tokens should be used (filler tokens should be used during both evaluation and fine-tuning).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnx6b83wch51"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefx6b83wch51">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ideally, the measure of no-CoT capabilities would be especially focused on the most concerning no-CoT capabilities such as subversion capabilities or ability to perform latent communication between agents. While ECI is a reasonable baseline proposal for how to aggregate scores over multiple datasets, any metric where we have good reason to think there will be a relatively clean predictable trend given a similar increase in underlying capabilities could work.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncmm4bshm3iu"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcmm4bshm3iu">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The change in no-CoT ECI versus overall ECI could be compared to the historical trend—how much overall ECI tends to increase for each increment of no-CoT ECI on the same datasets—as a proxy for how some change (or set of changes) relatively enables latent reasoning.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqpar64bbvsl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqpar64bbvsl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With redactions for IP as appropriate.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnre2vt7xhm58"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefre2vt7xhm58">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evaluations should also cover whether, even without elicitation, these models clearly exhibit reduced monitorability. E.g., in cases where the models exhibit misalignment: is this less visible in their chain-of-thought?</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngpx5nhxdsgi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgpx5nhxdsgi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qualitatively new techniques for scaling serial depth are another plausible trigger for needing a monitorability assessment.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnuve68qvm5id"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefuve68qvm5id">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Any modifications to the policy should be made public.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4ruq42ezg3w"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4ruq42ezg3w">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, the rough fraction of R&amp;D compute and number of researchers.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hLPGv8QjPcNLtDp3A/proposal-for-tracking-the-effects-of-architecture-on#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [AI #185: Preference Cascade](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tFmtz9HW6c2X9dw2B/ai-185-preference-cascade)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>The world of AI is inside my OODA loop. Even if I can process all the incoming information and sculpt it into posts, and even using Saturday and Sunday as flex slots, I don’t have enough days of the week to post all the posts that need posting.</p>
<p>That was already true. There was already a preference cascade happening where people finally were admitting that they thought AI might well kill everyone.</p>
<p>Then Jacob Coxon resigned from Anthropic, rang the warning bells and turned that cascade into an avalanche.</p>
<p>Now that is what everyone is talking about. Finally, everyone is actually saying the thing, out loud. I plan to cover that in its own post soon.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25595"></span>


</div>
<p>There are several things in the weekly that, in a normal week, would get their own coverage. Senator Sanders and Representative Casar introduced an outright ban on superintelligence and I have to remind myself that happened this week. Suddenly it is not so crazy to think such a thing might pass.</p>
<p>So here’s what I’ve already posted about so far since the last weekly:</p>
<ol>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/claude-fable-51-and-mythos-51-the?r=67wny"><strong>Claude Fable and Mythos 5.1: The System Card.</strong></a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/claude-mythos-51-and-fable-51-capabilities?r=67wny"><strong>Claude Fable and Mythos 5.1: Capabilities</strong></a>.</li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/openai-and-the-wiki-incident?r=67wny"><strong>OpenAI and the Wiki Incident.</strong></a>
<ol>
<li>Reports of OpenAI agents compromising additional Wikis continue to stream in, and OpenAI is continuing not to be the one to disclose them.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/an-alien-mind-jakub-pachocki-warns?r=67wny"><strong>An Alien Mind: Jakub Pachocki Warns Us.</strong></a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/astra-is-hard-to-monitor?r=67wny"><strong>Astra is Hard to Monitor</strong></a>.</li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/gpt-6-astra-the-system-card-alignment?r=67wny"><strong>GPT-6 Astra: The System Card, Alignment and What Comes Next.</strong></a></li>
</ol>
<p>Anthropic’s Claude Fable 5.1 is a very good model. OpenAI’s GPT-6 Astra is also a very good model, and a bigger improvement. Try both, see what works for you where.</p>
<p><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/openai-and-the-wiki-incident?r=67wny"><strong>The HuggingFace OpenAI saga got a prequel</strong></a>, as it turns out that there was a ‘Wiki Incident’ prior to the hack, which OpenAI decided not to disclose, that in some key ways changes our interpretation of the timeline.</p>
<p><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/an-alien-mind-jakub-pachocki-warns?r=67wny"><strong>OpenAI Chief Scientist Jakub Pachocki</strong></a> warned us that capabilities are developing rapidly, alignment is not keeping pace and monitorability is eroding fast. We will need to find ways to cooperate and slow down, or we are all cooked. This was a very good essay.</p>
<p>The Astra system card and several other OpenAI sources had lots of important and useful information. But the Astra release announcement, and some parts of the system card and Jakub’s essay, treated the evidence as being far less alarming than it is, and overclaiming on alignment concerns in ways that make me worried they don’t understand the dangers, both on alignment and monitorability.</p>
<p>Astra remains monitorable, contrary to some false alarms. but it is substantially less monitorable than Sol, in ways I do not think can be explained only by the increase in its capabilities. Right now OpenAI is extremely dependent on CoT monitoring, everyone else depends on it quite a lot as well, and it looks like it may not last much longer, and that Astra already is on the edge of steganographic capabilities and can do substantial obfuscation if and only if it thinks you would think it is up to no good. And Astra will ask the question previous misaligned AIs wouldn’t, as in it will not cheat in situations where it would expect to be caught.</p>
<p>Astra is substantially more aligned than Sol in the sense of what I call ‘mundane alignment,’ or the practical day to day use of the model. One might also call it ‘prosaic alignment.’ That is very different from alignment that scales, or that matters at the highest stakes, the one that ultimately counts, sometimes called ‘super alignment.’</p>
<p>That still leaves the pending standard post covering <strong>Astra’s Capabilities, </strong>as well as coverage of the Millennium Prize where <strong>an AI solved Navier-Stokes less then two weeks after OpenAI started training it</strong>, and we saw Anthropic and OpenAI unable to get along even there.</p>
<p>I also have not had opportunity to examine <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents">Anthropic’s alignment assessment of recent cybersecurity incidents</a>, or to offer my thoughts around <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qTtMXuFvgFtWzWpKQ/alex-mallen-s-shortform?commentId=5GyxSnTgPAkXkdfEn">Alex Mallen’s excellent post</a>.</p>
<p>Tentative schedule:</p>
<ol>
<li>Friday: Coxon and the Preference Cascade.</li>
<li>Saturday: The Millennium Prize and the new OpenAI model being trained.</li>
<li>Sunday: Astra Capabilities.</li>
</ol>
<p>After that, we will see where we are.</p>

<h4>Language Models Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p>Remember when people still tried to doubt that AI coding massively sped people up? Ruben Bloom, who was part of the METR uplift study that found devs were not much accelerated by AI, <a href="https://x.com/ruben_bloom/status/2095615757128438228">now reports he’s seeing unambiguous 10-50x speedups on projects</a>. This was before Astra.</p>
<p><a href="https://t.co/ryYnDEAU6J">Claude submits a formal Lean proof of Fermat’s Last Theorem</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/NathanpmYoung/status/2096021132650672334">Nathan <img alt="🔎" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/WaDFCrd6KEwojLXgj/q6yrv7y3f3ho1oz162og" style="height: 1em;" /></a>: Can we have a speedrunning-style thing where the game is now to prove theorems in fewer characters of lean?</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Tim_Hua_/status/2096691623656833167">Jakub Pachocki incidentally said</a> in <a href="https://openai.com/index/an-alien-mind/"><em>An Alien Mind</em></a> that OpenAI could make the models better at math, but is choosing not to focus on that. Which means that the math progress we see is well short of what we could be seeing.</p>


<h4>Language Models Don’t Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p>A zen koan: Are you sure that isn’t the problem, sir?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/yacineMTB/status/2096731177507811406">kache</a> (reviewing Astra): I am shocked how little of my personal progress in everything at life was blocked by intelligence.</p></blockquote>
<p>Could we ‘obviously assume’ that before?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Raemon777/status/2096500565241119116">Raymond Arnold</a>: Up until now, I’ve been assuming the latest models are basically safe to use. I think we can no longer obviously assume that.</p></blockquote>
<p>We are talking price. It is obviously safe on a personal level to use Astra or Fable 5.1 for ordinary chat tasks, indeed safer than using previous models.</p>
<p>The question is, at what point are you worried about using Astra for agentic tasks, or giving Astra access to sufficient credentials to do serious damage, and how does that compare to our trust in Sol or Fable and so on.</p>
<p>I would be nonzero nervous about using Astra in particular for sufficiently ‘high stakes’ tasks and take additional precautions at non-trivial cost, but I would be fine with using it for ordinary agentic tasks, including coding tasks.</p>

<h4>How To Tell a Fable</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/daniel_mac8/status/2094955084123910297">Dan McAteer</a>: 5 Fable 5.1 optimizations you can use *NOW* <a href="https://x.com/RLanceMartin/status/2094854835854295296">from @RLanceMartin</a></p>
<p>1. Set effort to ‘low’<br />
2. Run ‘/claude-api cost-optimize’<br />
3. Run ‘claude-api prompt-audit’<br />
4. Change effort mid-conversation w/o cache hit<br />
5. Update Fable 5.1 API config w/ ‘claude-api migrate’</p></blockquote>
<p>A lot of people were complaining about token use, and I suspect effort level is being set too high for many Claude Code purposes. There is a time and a place for max effort but it likely should not be standard. Astra also seems to not need high effort levels for many tasks from what I’ve seen.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KentonVarda/status/2096818173609718053">Fable is reported to be super into ‘red checks</a>,’ meaning confirming your new test would have failed without your changes. This appears to be a (good) neologism.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ClaudeDevs/status/2097369738968195513">Anthropic offers advice on prompt caching</a>, instructions and effort levels to improve performance and reduce cost.</p>

<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2096429509088293327">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Regressed on my fiction-plotting benchmarks. Haven’t tried it yet on my previous write-comprehensibly problem that blocked against Fable 5.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Deepfaketown and Botpocalypse Soon</h4>


<p>These estimates seem reasonable to me for a general election, if ‘consult’ means being genuinely unsure who to vote for, rather than ‘asked the LLM about election things’:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/robinhanson/status/2096613684768350589">Robin Hanson</a>: Median estimates: 12% of 2028 US pres. voters will consult an LLM, 78% will do what it recommends.</p></blockquote>
<p>Most voters in America already know which party they will vote for in 2028 if conditions do not radically change, and margins are very small. So if 28% of the 12%, or ~3%, are actually persuadable in this way, that is quite a lot. But you can’t put your finger on that scale too aggressively, or it would be obvious and backfire, including politically. So the direct impact is unlikely to be so large. I would worry more about the general assumed attitudes seeping into discourse.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.theargumentmag.com/p/the-core-anti-pangram-constituency">Kelsey Piper offers perspective on those opposed to Pangram</a>. Sometimes the obvious explanation is the correct one.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.theargumentmag.com/p/the-core-anti-pangram-constituency">Kelsey Piper</a>: A core constituency for arguments like <em>Wired</em>’s is “writers” who are using AI to write their posts but resent this fact being made public. They stand to lose from AI detectors because AI detectors call them out.</p>
<p>When Substack announced a partnership with Pangram, a sizable fraction of the platform’s authors rose up in outrage. Some people had experience with other AI detectors that didn’t work, and some felt that AI writing ought to be encouraged — both of which are fair enough. But the fury was fueled by authors passionately insisting that Pangram&nbsp;<a href="https://twlacrosse.substack.com/p/pangram-can-bite-me">kept wrongly accusing them</a>&nbsp;on&nbsp;<a href="https://twlacrosse.substack.com/p/pangram-can-bite-me/comment/299935591">almost every post they published</a>.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p><em>Wired</em>&nbsp;quotes Sam Illingworth, a professor of critical AI literacy at Edinburgh Napier University, who said, “I don’t think [AI detectors] work. … I think that detectors are prejudiced against certain people.” This spring, he said that he&nbsp;<a href="https://theslowai.substack.com/p/anthropic-pentagon-ai-safety-blacklist">used AI for research and editing</a>; this summer he acknowledged that in addition to research and editing, he also uses it “<a href="https://theslowai.substack.com/p/substack-ai-detection-witch-hunt">now and then for a turn of phrase I keep</a>.” Pangram&nbsp;<a href="https://www.pangram.com/history/f9822572-610f-4cbb-9ec5-780cbbe6bfeb?ucc=ODUJFRiKp4d">informs us that</a>&nbsp;<a href="https://www.pangram.com/history/dcb233e6-e1bf-4138-925f-d4ca3ed4160d?ucc=ODUJFRiKp4d">most of his posts are 100% AI-written</a>&nbsp;(some are mixed AI-and-human) and that&nbsp;<a href="https://www.pangram.com/history/9bbdb779-e5a0-4f82-a317-31b577c6f8a4?ucc=ODUJFRiKp4d">100% AI-written posts started appearing last year</a>.</p></blockquote>
<p>Look, I like Pangram, but in most cases if they’re objecting to Pangram, you do not need Pangram to know that an AI wrote the passage, because it looks like this:</p>
<blockquote><p>Illingworth is not alone in insisting the detector does not work. “It’s a Monday night in late July, and I’m pasting my own Substack draft into an AI detector for the first time. Not because I’m worried. Because I’m curious. I wrote every word of it myself, the way I write everything, out loud in my head first, in a rhythm that still carries the accent of a language I learned to think in before I learned to write in this one,” begins&nbsp;<a href="https://aibyshrabony.substack.com/p/i-set-the-thresholds-where-false">one Substack post by an author indignant that Pangram flags her writing as AI</a>. “That’s the part I had to sit with before I could write this.”</p>
<p>If you, like me, have worked with AIs a lot, you won’t even need Pangram there. The&nbsp;<em>author</em>&nbsp;of that piece is AI. It is obviously AI! I checked 10 other posts on the blog, and they were also obviously AI!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2096807862945325113">Kelsey Piper</a>: There are a couple categories of objection to Pangram. Some people think AI writing should be fine – fair enough. But some insist that it’s bad, and so is trying to catch it. I disagree.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2096817992114081866">Kelsey Piper</a>: A friend elsewhere wrote: ‘strong aura of the small child, hands covered in cookie crumbs and with chocolate smeared across their face, loudly insisting their sibling ate the cookies’</p></blockquote>
<p>There are some exceptions. I do not think there are all that many.</p>
<p>I agree with this, as well, provided my own ear doesn’t confirm Pangram’s story:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2096837858766233813">Kelsey Piper</a>: My own rule of thumb is that if someone’s writing usually doesn’t flag as AI and one or two pieces do, I’ll give them the benefit of the doubt. But if all of someone’s writing is AI, they aren’t the unluckiest person in the world.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Levels of Friction</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/tweetsbenedict/status/2097104968440217705">High demand restaurant reservations are not in equilibrium</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Cdpetty/status/2096966373931090336">Clayton Petty</a>: I regret to inform you all the the logical end state for restaurant reservations is a bidding system where agents can big against each other for spots they want. Free reservations will no longer be a thing in 3yrs</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jbahrdestefano/status/2096676801204404604">JC Bahr-de Stefano</a> (AI-written): Totally fair action from Resy tbh just asked Instinct for its activity log and holy hell lol</p>
<p>“Total: roughly 200 API requests per hour, around the clock</p>
<p>1. 4 Charles availability sweep every 10 minutes, 24/7 since Friday morning – each run hit their API ~17-19 times (one per date in a 21-day window). ~100-115 calls/hour<br />
2. Every morning at 9am drop time, a 2.5-minute burst polling every 0.4 seconds – 200-375 requests per drop<br />
3. Occasional session-token refreshes”</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tFmtz9HW6c2X9dw2B/rf2bjg2en3fz4t9dpnqj" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jbahrdestefano/status/2096685175371374677">JC Bahr-de Stefano</a>: The whole experience begs some interesting questions ab the future of the internet</p>
<p>Like if everyone has an agent running errands for them, every site w/ scarce inventory has a few paths:</p>
<p>1. Ban bots entirely<br />
2. Build for agents w/ their own rate limits, auth, and endpoints (e.g., recognize this is JC’s agent)<br />
3. Ship their own agent so the only bot on the platform is theirs (e.g., I tell Resy I want a table for 2 at these places on this date and its system handles for me)<br />
4. Let the bots in and run it as an auction – the reservation goes to whoever is willing to pay the most<br />
5. Something else entirely?</p>
<p>And there will be a good amount of pain + a lot of accounts are gonna get banned while everyone figures it out</p></blockquote>
<p>What definitely does not work are race conditions. Alas, race conditions are what the top restaurants largely use, by offering valuable goods for free at a specified time, first come first serve. Won’t work.</p>
<p>I would of course choose the auction. It is the only fair way and it maximizes profits, and you can keep some slots out of the auction to give to loyal customers or VIPs or walk-ins and so on, as you see fit.</p>
<p>If they cannot bear to do that, the missing suggestion is a lottery. You buy a Resy membership for (let’s say) $50 a year with an ID attached, you indicate which in-demand reservations you want with a priority order, and when you win and choose to keep the reservation you have to show up in person or you forfeit your membership deposit. Reservations without excess demand are free. Maybe have progressive levels where better memberships let you enter more often or have better odds.</p>
<p>Then, if someone wants to use a bot to set their prioritization orders, that’s fine.</p>
<p>I have never paid for a restaurant reservation, but I would if it was straightforward to do it, I could pay the restaurant directly and it was above board rather than some sketchy weird secondary market.</p>


<h4>Cyber Lack of Security</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/08/us/politics/calif-ai-worm-wechat-hack.html?unlocked_article_code=1._1A.QT8o._cws217pZt60&amp;smid=nytcore-android-share">You want to know how bad it could get</a>? Pretty bad. As in, ‘take over a large portion of the phones in China in rapid succession’ levels of bad, based on a worm built in a week.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2097533453537300550">Peter Wildeford<img alt="🇺🇸" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/WmxS7dbHuxzxFei64/qyak3qhbg12zgddskl2o" style="height: 1em;" /><img alt="🚀" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6YxtwzF9ZEXxJ5wij/on26fpadxwlhds1n9fca" style="height: 1em;" /></a>: um wut… NYT reports:</p>
<p>– Researchers at a security firm used AI to build a self-propagating WeChat worm. You get a phone call. If you answer or even just let it ring, your phone is infected.<br />
– The worm was built in a little over a week with significant AI assistance and acceleration.<br />
– It compromises your account, reads and sends your messages, makes calls, and then auto-spreads to everyone you’ve saved as a friend.<br />
– Chained with other bugs they say it could have fully compromised the phone.<br />
– Tencent has patched it and says no users were affected. WeChat has 1.4 billion monthly users, nearly all in China.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/08/us/politics/calif-ai-worm-wechat-hack.html">Dustin Volz</a> (NYTimes): Calif said the attack, which it named WeWorm, was the first known computer worm — a type of malicious software that can leap from machine to machine on its own absent human help — that could spread across Apple’s iOS and Google’s Android operating systems without needing a victim to click or tap on anything.</p>
<p>So-called zero-click attacks are different, and far more lethal, than standard phishing emails and texts. They are considered especially pernicious because they are so hard to defend against, given that they do not require a victim to step into a digital booby trap.</p></blockquote>
<p>You could say ‘oh come on there’s no way you can take over my phone based on a call I did not even pick up that does not make any sense’ but it turns out, well, yeah. You think we will simply ‘patch all the vulnerabilities’?</p>
<p>A logical personal response would be to remove from your phone any apps, especially communication apps, that you do not need.</p>
<p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/09/numbers-numbers-numbers.html?utm_source=rss&amp;utm_medium=rss&amp;utm_campaign=numbers-numbers-numbers">People being this asleep at the wheel about cybersecurity is very bad news</a>. Supposedly Very Serious People are predicting only 20% of cyberattacks in 2027 will involve AI? Is this a joke? Yes, these are numbers, but the secret of many numbers is some guys just made them up. Those guys are often idiots.</p>
<p>I am mostly going to abide by my promise not to cover quixotic ‘<a href="https://x.com/tylercowen/status/2095868117649952809">stop getting rich guys, why are you not short the market, and why aren’t you responding to my blog posts about the real risks with proper journal articles</a>?’ rants, from Tyler Cowen or otherwise, <a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/09/more-numbers-for-the-hugging-face-incident.html?utm_source=rss&amp;utm_medium=rss&amp;utm_campaign=more-numbers-for-the-hugging-face-incident">but I did find this one interesting</a> because it illustrates that the market is just, quite frankly, not good at this.</p>
<p>The market often is so clueless it reacts the wrong way purely on its own terms. Remember when DeepSeek r1 came out, and Nvidia share prices fell in response to the news that their chips were highly useful (and that time yes I did buy)?</p>
<p>So let’s look at the reaction to the attack on HuggingFace. Why does Tyler Cowen think that, when it is clear we will need a lot more cybersecurity, that their services will be highly useful, prices of those who sell such services will fall rather than rise?</p>
<p>My instinct was that of course cybersecurity firm shares should by default go up. Incumbents will be in good position to use top AIs well. <a href="https://chatgpt.com/s/cx_6a9c0708efa08191a36276251caeb4bd">Astra agrees</a>. <a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2017/08/can-short-apocalypse.html">Fable agrees</a>. This goes well beyond the true ‘<a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2017/08/can-short-apocalypse.html">you cannot short the apocalypse.</a>’ This is ‘the market can stay crazy longer than you can stay pretty much anything.’ It is also ‘the price you are citing often does not mean what you think it means, even on its own terms.’</p>
<p><a href="https://www.chinatalk.media/p/cyber-apocalypse-now">Joshua Saxe is interviewed on all things HuggingFace attack</a>. We should be alarmed, he says, but not surprised. Security practices are not good at the labs, it is the Wild West, but releasing more models faster is good for cybersecurity because defenders use AI a lot and attackers use it less. I see profound failures of imagination and thinking ahead throughout.</p>
<p>Saxe says that if you went to sleep eighteen months ago your idea of AI cyber would be totally wrong, then does not follow through on the implication. He says AI is defense dominant, mostly through backward looking in places where the attackers lack ‘the juice’ and aren’t trying so hard. But he is <a href="https://x.com/joshua_saxe/status/2096082715208519739">miles ahead of many of his security friends</a>, and at least trying, so he is warning them that ‘misalignment risk is not a conspiracy or a marketing stunt’ and this is a sentence we still need to utter in September 2026.</p>
<p>I did learn that cybercrime is already 0.5%-1% of global GDP purely in terms of damages. Which means it costs us far more than that, since most cost is opportunity cost of having to defend against it. Saxe does agree this might get a lot worse.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.chinatalk.media/p/cyber-apocalypse-now">Joshua Saxe</a>: In terms of what happens next — it’s reasonable to assume that over the next few years cybercrime gets a lot worse. A simple mental model: software engineers have gotten maybe three times more productive thanks to coding agents. Maybe cyber criminals get three times more productive at breaking into networks.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is an example of failure to look forward. If there is a 3x productivity boost now, the correct assumption is that in the future the productivity boost, in places it carries over, will a lot more than 3x. Indeed, given how this scales, a better model might be in practice 10x or 100x or even 1,000x or more. As in, once I have an attack procedure, I can probe every target with one click, provided I can afford the compute, whereas right now most attacks that would succeed are never attempted. Whereas each defender will still have to protect their own house. Good luck, defenders.</p>
<p>Another way one might model this is that right now, Saxe is right that attackers largely use social engineering as the enabler, because it is more efficient. Then you cross a threshold when that stops being true, or where the AI can do the social engineering.</p>
<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/daybreak-for-frontline-defenders/">OpenAI commits $1 billion</a> in credits to Daybreak for Frontline Defenders, to help enhance cybersecurity. Excellent.</p>


<h4>A Young Lady’s Illustrated Primer</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/Kalshi/status/2095859431544422859">Los Angeles bans students from using AI in public schools on district devices.</a> That is not all that meaningful of a ban when you put it that way.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2095589104620797997">Harvard dean David Deming suggests that we accept or even encourage</a> <a href="https://www.thecrimson.com/article/2026/9/3/humanities-ai-pushback-deming/">AI use for any assignments that cannot fit within a proctored exam window</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.thecrimson.com/article/2026/9/3/humanities-ai-pushback-deming/">Abigail S. Gerstein and Amann S. Mahajan, Crimson Staff Writers</a> (The Harvard Crimson): He added that the approach would help affiliates avoid “the AI-detection business because it damages trust between faculty and students.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Tyler_A_Harper/status/2095535173194420445">Tyler Austin Harper</a>: Here’s the relevant part of the Harvard dean’s email, in which he suggests (“modestly”) that professors teaching writing intensive courses should encourage AI use. And of course this gets dressed up in woke moralizing about policing students and damaging “trust.” End times stuff.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2095589104620797997">Jack</a>: I used AI tools to the fullest allowed extent during my education, but I think encouraging more than very cautious use of them during education is a mistake. If you care about any of the underlying skills, AI is likely to supplant, not enhance, development of those skills.</p></blockquote>
<p>If you are unwilling to call out cheating, as in use Pangram and fail students on that basis, and you are not good enough to otherwise give bad grades in response to AI use, then what choice do you have? Your alternative is a broken eval.</p>


<h4>They Took Our Jobs</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/JohnCassidy/status/2097001178856870302">Unemployment holds steady at 4.1%</a>, now continuously causing <a href="https://t.co/wyhDzL0F29">headlines like John Cassidy</a> asking ‘Has the AI Job Apocalypse Been Postponed?’ That depends on when you scheduled it. My expectations are unchanged, that unemployment rates will hold steady until we hit critical mass, because we still have a reserve of ‘shadow jobs’ and displacement is not yet too fast to handle. If we keep seeing this rate of exponential growth in AI use then we might not have to wait so much longer.</p>
<p><a href="https://asteriskmag.com/issues/15/after-work-we-ll-have-each-other">Clara Collier sees the basic human need not as work</a>, but as relational. There have been plenty of social classes that did not do anything we would consider ‘productive work,’ and yet they still work. The work is intrigue, it is gossip, it is social relations, it is filling a role, whether or not it accomplishes anything beyond that. The system exists largely to police any who would do anything outside a narrow range of approved activities.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://asteriskmag.com/issues/15/after-work-we-ll-have-each-other">Clara Collier</a> (Asterisk): When the machines can do everything else, our relationships will be what’s left.</p>
<p>… That’s the natural consequence of all these relationalist predictions — a world where our connections to other humans are what give us structure, identity, and meaning.</p>
<p>I am afraid they’re right.</p></blockquote>
<p>That strikes me as the correct attitude. One can also worry, Oscar Wilde style, that perhaps the only thing worse is not having human relationships in the first place.</p>
<p>The main flaw here is the presumption that the future will look like the past. Even if we presume an idle rich world, where humans have material abundance, many other things will have changed. At minimum, we will have these AIs, and they will be interesting in lots of ways, and they will manage all this intrigue and relational conflict a lot better than we could without them.</p>
<p>A lot of us are currently in the ‘we are more productive so we work all the time’ mode of AI usage, as illustrated by Jessica Tillipman in <a href="https://jessicatillipman.com/my-family-hates-ai/">My Family Hates AI</a>, or rather that <a href="https://x.com/JTillipman/status/2096962636428525783">she’s constantly talking to AI while doing other things</a>, so she can be ready to then do the writing herself later. The key is to not let AI write or directly edit. So much more productive, but never a quiet mind.</p>
<p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/09/how-much-redistribution-will-ai-require.html">If ordinary humans are sufficiently in charge to do redistribution</a> and growth rates are high, transfers can make the population materially well off even if labor share of income drops to zero, without impacting said growth too much. Sure.</p>
<p>But Alex Tabarrok’s assumption in the linked post that little redistribution would be required rests on the idea that in the ‘terminal’ state human labor would enjoy some equilibrium nonzero share of income, and things like ‘cut the work week in half’ are meaningful. I don’t see any reason to presume this. The conclusion is baked into the scenarios, which are decidedly not AGI pilled let alone ASI pilled.</p>
<p><a href="https://t.co/YrN0wG6UGS">The NYT pitchbot cannot keep pace</a>. No notes.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TrevMcKendrick/status/2096762469784137756">Trevor McKendrick</a>: “<a href="https://t.co/YrN0wG6UGS">AI has destroyed Kenyan jobs because American college students no longer hire them to write fake college essays</a>” is not a framing I expected even the NYT to have</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tFmtz9HW6c2X9dw2B/uyngtlxddcw71j6kphpr" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div><figcaption></figcaption></figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tysonbrody/status/2097030666030350774">tyson brody</a>: I think you can connect the decline on overall literacy and the return of ‘orality’ to the fact so many people presume every news story is explicitly moralistic and not just ‘here is an interesting thing happening in the world today that you may not know about’</p></blockquote>
<p>This is a crisis in Kenyan education. How will they learn if no one is paying them to write the essays?</p>


<h4>Anthropic Offers Economic Scenarios</h4>


<p>The Econ Scenarios are remarkably not superintelligence pilled. This is largely a ‘how does AI as an ordinary technology impact the economy?’ toy model exercise. All that AI does in these scenarios is automate and augment particular tasks.</p>
<p>I’ll quickly go over it, as it’s a fun little toy, but I don’t think it tells us much.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/institute/econ-scenarios">Anthropic</a>: While our <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/economic-index">Economic Index</a> measures how AI is being used across the economy right now, this scenario explorer is about looking ahead. Based on our technical report, <a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/files/4zrzovbb/website/cf58f84d46a4a76bf5a5b039ac695fba6b80041c.pdf">Economic Scenarios for Transformative AI</a> (Korinek et al., 2026), this explorer gives you a chance to find out what the economy might look like as AI continues to get more capable.</p></blockquote>
<p>They offer three scenarios: Modest, substantial and extreme. This is represented by three lines on a graph, with different slopes, until the extreme version has AI expanding growth rates to 15% a year, alongside rising unemployment due to automation of knowledge work.</p>
<p>They present the scenarios as tasks augmented or automated, with new tasks created. The internet is a series of tubes, and the economy is a series of tasks.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tFmtz9HW6c2X9dw2B/nffzmbxhbdr1uu4blv0x" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>The ‘extreme’ scenario is both extreme and also not that extreme. All it is saying is that ~45% of tasks get automated by 2030. The ‘nature of life’ has not changed, and not that many jobs are displaced, including almost no non-knowledge workers.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tFmtz9HW6c2X9dw2B/futkezwspn6ba1n2xl6w" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>13.5% of workers is a lot, and they have 8.3% of all workers still out in the cold. This doubtless underestimates how many other workers get displaced by the knowledge workers. As in, a lot of knowledge workers that get displaced would enter the non-knowledge pool and compete for existing jobs, and often be overqualified for them.</p>
<p>My presumption is that under a lot of growth, if we assume non-knowledge workers are not substantially displaced (which is a very false assumption), and wages are either falling or at least rising a lot slower than growth, then we should have more than enough ‘shadow jobs’ that we are happy to pay people to do, and doubtless there will be more as growth and capability create new opportunities. The question is, how many knowledge or other workers will be willing to accept those jobs, that previously were not attractive enough for anyone?</p>
<p>But this makes me highly skeptical of the idea that real wages for non-knowledge work would rise, except insofar as goods and services become cheaper.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tFmtz9HW6c2X9dw2B/clb3jufciywazsuw1fjw" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Suppose non-knowledge pay rises 33%. Well, 8% of the workforce is supposedly still idle, having not yet reallocated. Why are they so unwilling to take these jobs, such that we need wages to rise 33%? Meanwhile, remaining knowledge worker pay is down 11%. You would expect huge attempts to migrate from knowledge to non-knowledge work, even for those whose jobs are intact.</p>
<p>I get that there is a large penalty in the model for switching occupations, but this seems rather extreme, as does the amount of required transitional time.</p>
<p>Anthropic thinks labor share of income will still drop modestly in those scenarios, from a 0.6% drop in the modest scenario to 14.8% drop in the extreme one, which would reduce labor share down to 45.2%.</p>
<p><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/files/4zrzovbb/website/cf58f84d46a4a76bf5a5b039ac695fba6b80041c.pdf">The actual mechanics behind all this are in the PDF</a>. I had Astra and Fable dig into the paper.</p>
<blockquote><p>Fable: The skeleton is Acemoglu-Restrepo task automation bolted onto a Jones semi-endogenous growth block and a Mortensen-Pissarides search/matching labor market, with one big structural cut: the economy is two islands, “cognitive” occupations (62% of employment: management, professional, sales, office) and everyone else, and AI only ever touches the first island. No robotics, which is also why they stop at 2030.​</p></blockquote>
<p>It’s a cool series of modeling choices, but fundamentally it’s an ass pull, full of assumptions that are obviously false regardless of the dials, the methods of impact are strictly bounded, and it bakes in the idea that AI is not transformational.</p>


<h4>Get Involved</h4>


<p><a href="https://coefficientgiving.org/tailwind/">Coefficient Giving launches Project Tailwind, a call for ambitious AI safety initiatives</a>. Founders are the bottleneck, not funding. Don’t worry that you are taking funds away from someone else. They are offering pre-seed funding up to $2 million, or seed funding up to $20 million, or even scaling funding in the $20 million+ range.</p>
<p><a href="https://coefficientgiving.org/tailwind/initiatives/">They have extensive lists of suggested topics</a> and are open to suggestions. It runs the gamut of pretty much everything an EA-style AI safety approach has ever funded, including everything from direct technical work to meta-level projects.</p>
<p>Take them at their word that they want to move the money out the door. If you have something worth doing, that can accept their money, ask away. You can fill out a form <a href="https://coefficientgiving.org/tailwind/get-involved/">here</a>, if you do then tell them I sent you.</p>
<p>Unfortunately, I am in a position where accepting their funding would impact how my efforts are viewed, so I have decided I cannot myself apply.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dwarkesh_sp/status/2097707668920205580">Dwarkesh Patel points out that time is running short, and funding is not</a>. If you want to tackle AI existential risk at this point, or even AI normal risk, you probably want to try and Do The Thing directly rather than have it be a side effect of Doing Business. You may still have time to become a load-bearing institution if you start now.</p>
<p>The flip side is that you can zero-to-one shockingly quickly now thanks to AI.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/FioraStarlight/status/2095936456795849031">You may wish to hire Fiora Starlight</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/GarrisonLovely/status/2096306697119662283">Garrison Lovely’s Signal is Garrison.06</a> if you want to share information that the public needs to know. I am also happy to help you break news, and will give you whatever level of confidentiality you request. I function on a much more source-friendly set of rules than typical journalists.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sparrowinfra/status/2097717365911757266?s=46">Takeoff 2026 is a new conference on the governance of AI</a>, that will take place in Washington D.C. from November 13-15. They have an excellent lineup, and I estimate a 75% chance I will be able to go.</p>
<p>If you’re looking to pivot from an AI lab into AI safety, where to go? Here are some suggestions, the list looks solid:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/OwainEvans_UK/status/2097778368452423724">Owain Evans</a>: If you’re thinking of moving into AI safety, there are various excellent non-profit research organizations. They generally pay very well and some try to match AI lab salaries. They have generous compute budgets (and increasing fast).</p>
<p>Here’s a quick list of those I’m most familiar with:<br />
@redwood_ai<br />
@ApolloResearch<br />
@farairesearch<br />
METR<br />
ARC<br />
UK AISI (UK Government, lower pay but very valuable)<br />
Resolution<br />
@CAIS<br />
My organization (<a href="http://truthful.ai)/">http://truthful.ai)</a> will also run a hiring round soon.</p></blockquote>

<p><a href="https://x.com/MariusHobbhahn/status/2095557648645075386">Watcher Live from Apollo Research</a>,<a href="https://watcher.apolloresearch.ai/blog/announcing-watcher-live/"> a real-time coding agent monitor</a> that blocks dangerous actions to prevent data leaks, repo deletions and scope overreach. They are happy to have you or the labs steal from it.</p>

<h4>Quiet Speculations</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/davidshor/status/2095721539916206200">The recurring pattern is:</a></p>
<ol>
<li>Person, here Ajeya Cotra, says sensible things.</li>
<li>Person, here Itai Sher, says this person made predictions I find absurd, therefore you should ignore everything she says.</li>
<li>Person, here David Shor, points out that the first person’s predictions seem unusually accurate so far and keep coming true, if anything they have been too conservative, whereas those whose predictions sound measured keep being wrong.</li>
</ol>
<p>Alas, step 4 is ‘<a href="https://x.com/itaisher/status/2095468056201871397">the person in step 2 learns nothing</a> and we repeat the cycle.’ Here the excuse is ‘well of course the crazy people have better predictions, they are paying more attention to the situation.’ You would think this would then cause a ‘huh, I notice all the people paying more attention and thus better calibrated than I am about current events keep making these predictions that sound absurd to me anyway’ and not finishing it meme-style with ‘no it is the so-far accurate predictors who pay more attention who are wrong, purely because their other predictions sound absurd.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/davidshor/status/2095872343520297060">David Shor</a>: I do think that people should update more from the fact that the people with crazy sounding conceptions of the future keep winning forecasting competitions on AI progress while people with more measured intuitions keep undershooting on hard quantitative benchmarks.</p>
<p>What’s really influenced me a lot was knowing a bunch of EA’s in the early/mid 2010’s and thinking they were crazy for being obsessed with pandemics and AI only for a pandemic to happen and then AI to happen too.</p>
<p>The pandemic thing was really funny – I had one EA on my team and we kept reprimanding him for trying to slip pandemic stuff into our polling deliverables in 2017 because he was extremely concerned that we were highly unprepared for a pandemic <img alt="🤷‍♂️" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/qtEgaxSFxYanT5QbJ/su2yvkkcxcjaq2mabeui" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/weidai11/status/2095907097762316756">Wei Dai</a>: Look at this from the other side (of someone trying to do long-horizon strategy): you spend most of your time having low status for being “obsessed” with something nobody else cares about, and then can only get points for being early, not for the details of your strategies/ideas, which we still can’t evaluate. (Did the early EAs/rationalists obsessed with AI safety make the situation better or worse, and how to divide up the credit among them? It’s genuinely hard to tell, even now.)</p></blockquote>
<p>Nate Silver is very correct here, I do not expect a plateau based on everything I am hearing from both labs, and Andrew is largely correct as well:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/NateSilver538/status/2096410803414987172">Nate Silver</a>: I don’t think we get to GPT-7 without either a plateau in the technology or something fairly seismic happening. “Seismic” includes many subcategories, not all of them bad. But the present course of rapid AI advancements while society is ~basically the same is running on fumes.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2096421741484769708">Andrew Curran</a>: This seismic change already exists in the system, from existing models. It just has not been realized yet (cost/implementation). Even if we somehow stopped completely today, what you describe will still happen.</p></blockquote>
<p>Here’s a good question:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndyMasley/status/2097191414098456956">Andy Masley</a>: A basic question people should ask is whether, if someone had predicted the abilities of the current models two years ago, you would have accused them of falling victim to insane sci-fi hype.</p></blockquote>
<p>The precise right question is, if someone predicted roughly this level of overall capabilities with vaguely this distribution, how you would have reacted.</p>
<p>So, I want to register my prediction, <a href="https://www.understandingai.org/p/why-humanoid-robots-wont-catch-up">in response to this article</a>…</p>

This makes a lot of people worry about mass unemployment. But @chi_t_williams wrote an amazing article explaining why it’ll take years — possibly decades — for robots to match humans.</p></blockquote>
