<h4>The Quest for Sane Regulations</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.ft.com/content/560e1c8b-f163-4fd6-b604-e905550ac870">Anthropic withheld Mythos 5.1 from UK AISI</a>, presumably on White House orders. This is an extremely disheartening move, especially after UK AISI found some important and disturbing misaligned behaviors in Mythos 5.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidshor/status/2097765310250074349">Giving auditors access to the AI labs and their systems is extremely popular</a>. The public favors outside experts licensed by the government doing this, rather than a direct government agency.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RepYassAnsari/status/2095921753595785390">Congresswoman Yassamin Ansari calls for Congressional hearings</a> on the HuggingFace attack and related incidents, and the creation of safeguards.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/03/technology/openai-hugging-face-hack.html">Congressman Suhas Subramanyam also pointed to the HuggingFace attack</a> as a potential catalyst for legislation.</p>
<p>Realistically, <a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2096039861870358698">I do not expect this Congress to do anything about AI</a>, except perhaps hold some hearings. <a href="https://x.com/JTillipman/status/2096200715660062784">Our government does not do things</a> at this point in election cycles. We might get some movement from the new Congress in 2027.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AlexBores/status/2095887465600913850">Alex Bores calls upon the major labs to establish a Mutually Agreed Pacing (MAP)</a> Framework, independent of government action. A key problem is that this could be considered anti-competitive behavior. The Federal Government needs to give the labs a targeted waiver of the antitrust rules if we want this to happen.</p>


<h4>The OpenAI Policy and Lobbying Department</h4>


<p>If OpenAI can change its stripes here, that would mean quite a lot.</p>
<p>The record has not been good. <a href="https://x.com/bradrcarson/status/2095937849396736134">OpenAI and Google continue, as far as we can tell, to oppose the Massachusetts bill</a> <a href="https://t.co/oaYEwfyAEA">that would require third party auditing</a>. Lehane claims that they are supporting the auditing provision, but I have not seen them say they are supporting the bill.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mattyglesias/status/2097273827038212607">As Matthew Yglesias says,</a> <a href="https://x.com/AlexBores/status/2097158668731244967">and Alex Bores emphasizes</a>, Jakub Pachocki’s essay <a href="https://openai.com/index/an-alien-mind/"><em>An Alien Mind</em></a> was excellent but is completely at odds with what OpenAI’s lobbyists and PACs have been up to, at least until yesterday. OpenAI has continuously represented that they are being helpful, while instead being mostly anti-helpful.</p>
<p>If OpenAI’s political activities line up with Pachocki’s statements, that would be a sign that OpenAI as a company actually means it. Until then, we have a problem.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/davidmanheim/status/2097646087246889386">David Manheim</a>: As long as Lehane is continuing to lobby against all of that coordination and any form of regulation, and Brockman is spending billions to fund a PAC fight such efforts, it’s much harder to take OpenAI’s “costly actions” seriously.</p></blockquote>
<p>The good news is that <a href="https://openai.com/index/ai-policy-window/">we have an announcement from Chris Lehane</a> and Astra (as in according to Pangram it was about 40% AI-written) that may be the start of this type of movement. They are trying to talk the talk.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://openai.com/index/ai-policy-window/">Chris Lehane, Chief Global Affairs Officer at Open</a>AI:</p>
<p><em>Here’s what we’re doing:</em></p>
<ol>
<li><strong><em>Pushing for mandatory national AI safety requirements. </em></strong><em>We want to work with Congress on mandatory, capability-based national AI safety regulation. </em></li>
<li><strong><em>Keeping up momentum in the states.</em></strong><em> Until Congress acts, we will continue supporting state legislation that strengthens the broader AI safety ecosystem. Today, we are announcing our support for four California bills: SB 813 on overall infrastructure for independent safety assessments, AB 1405 on AI-auditor standards, SB 1119 on protections for young people, and AB 1864 on safeguards against AI-enabled biological threats. </em></li>
<li><strong><em>Advancing industry-led standards.</em></strong><em> We will work with other frontier labs to advance frontier AI standards, building a voluntary effort now, with or without government support.</em></li>
<li><strong><em>Building global standards.</em></strong><em> We will advocate for compatible international approaches to measuring capabilities, managing risk, preserving human control, and determining when and how development should slow or stop, even if that means slowing the advancement of model capabilities.</em></li>
</ol>
<p>Our Chief Scientist Jakub Pachocki recently <a href="https://openai.com/index/an-alien-mind/">wrote⁠</a> that the rapid rise of machine intelligence, including the potential of recursive self-improvement, calls for “extreme caution.” OpenAI will continue pursuing technical solutions to alignment and monitoring, building defensive systems, and slowing development when necessary. But technical work inside individual labs will not be enough. We also need shared standards, including regarding when development should slow or stop.</p>
<p>… Astra’s capabilities, the early evidence of AI-driven research acceleration, and Jakub’s essay all point in the same direction: AI is advancing quickly, and policy needs to move with it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/boazbaraktcs/status/2097826190165774669">Boaz Barak</a> (OpenAI): Very happy about this from our chief of global affairs</p>
<p>Highlighting from above: <em>determining when and how development should slow or stop, even if that means slowing the advancement of model capabilities.</em></p></blockquote>
<p>That sentence is indeed a very good sign, and the lobbying department has now said:</p>
<blockquote><p>Chris Lehane: If we cannot meet certain safety bars without slowing down capability growth, we should prioritize the former.</p></blockquote>
<p>Not so pinned down, but quite a good start.</p>
<p>The post explicitly talks about preparing for recursive self-improvement, and about working with Congress on mandatory AI safety requirements. It also says a bunch of other applause light things around concentration of power and open weights and such, and of course ‘democracy.’</p>
<p>It also contains this whopper, in terms of the story they are trying to tell, even if there is a sense in which it is technically correct. At best this is Exact Words:</p>
<blockquote><p>OpenAI has supported California’s SB 53, New York’s RAISE Act, Illinois’s SB 315, and independent audits in the frontier safety legislation under consideration in Massachusetts.</p></blockquote>
<p>When you deal with Chris Lehane, you still deal with Chris Lehane.</p>
<p>The concrete move is the endorsement of the four California bills, all of which have already passed the California legislature, so the decision is fully up to Newsom. That is still a helpful time to endorse things.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://openai.com/index/ai-policy-window/">Chris Lehane, Chief Global Affairs Officer at Open</a>AI: Some of these bills we did not endorse in the past, and are now supporting after reconsidering in light of the recent jump in capabilities we have seen.</p>
<p>These bills are not a substitute for federal regulation. They are serious efforts that can protect people now, demonstrate what workable safeguards look like, and help build momentum for federal action.</p></blockquote>
<p>The overall call focuses on monitoring and reporting requirements, and they indicate a willingness to not let perfect be the enemy of the good.</p>
<blockquote><p>The AI policy window is open, for now. We intend to use it.</p>
<p>That means acting with urgency, humility, and a willingness to adapt. It means supporting serious proposals that materially raise the safety bar, even when they are not exactly what we would have designed. And it means strengthening the framework as the technology evolves.</p>
<p>No first step will be perfect. But the greater risk now is waiting too long to take one.​</p></blockquote>
<p>Chris Lehane calls upon the current Congress to act now. Alas, as a seasoned political agent, he knows that the current Congress is not going to be passing laws.</p>
<p>This may or may not be the start of a pivot to being helpful rather than anti-helpful.</p>
<p>Watch this space over the coming days and weeks. We shall see.</p>


<h4>Greetings From the Department of War</h4>


<p><a href="https://fedscoop.com/anthropic-government-responses-pentagon-battle-continues-claude/">Emil Michael is what we in the AI biz call a rogue agent</a>, continuing even now to go on social media to remind us that Anthropic is a ‘supply chain risk’ right after Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick says Anthropic and the government are ‘in tune together,’ lest someone get the wrong idea. He’s going to go down with the ship, and no one seems to have considered the ‘why don’t you fire him?’ solution.</p>
<p>Or, war by other means?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2097351750000976188">Andrew Curran</a>: Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent speaking live just said if China were to pull away from the US in AI nothing would matter, ‘We can’t pause’ and ‘There is no day after tomorrow if China wins’.</p></blockquote>
<p>The US is currently pulling away from China, in the sense that Astra and Fable 5.1 are far and away better than every other model, and OpenAI has an internal model substantially more advanced than Astra.</p>
<p>Why would there be no day after tomorrow if China wins? For the same reason there would be no day after tomorrow if America ‘wins’ without first solving multiple unsolved problems, including alignment. Because everyone would be dead.</p>
<p>Otherwise, if it’s merely about mundane AI, then why is China waking up tomorrow?</p>


<h4>Hugging The Face</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/03/technology/openai-hugging-face-hack.html">The New York Times had a report on the METR report</a> on the Hugging Face attack, by Dylan Freedman, that was top of their website for a few hours, <a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2096285241774395466">with a spread in the Business section</a> alongside <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/03/technology/openai-hugging-face-hacking.html?unlocked_article_code=1.-1A.MmsZ.EkbYzBQ-_jgv&amp;smid=url-share">another strong piece by Kevin Roose</a> explaining why actually yes the HuggingFace attack is a really big deal.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tFmtz9HW6c2X9dw2B/ometr8wh5blv0b3l2abi" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>The article takes things seriously, focuses on places worth focusing on, and gets its facts right. Good show. I’m fine with these things taking a few days, if they then both do a good job and get front page billing. Well done, New York Times.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tFmtz9HW6c2X9dw2B/ud2efw3tevvzqgeo2iay" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/robertwiblin/status/2095797837409706319">Rob Wiblin</a>: The METR/Redwood report is the top story on the New York Times home page the last few hours.</p>
<p>Piece takes as a given that the report is very troubling and notes that the reality could be even worse as the investigators weren’t given enough access to uncover everything.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.theguardian.com/commentisfree/2026/sep/08/openai-rogue-models-hugging-face-investigation">Mackenzie Arnold and Stephan Llerena call for a better investigation in The Guardian</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/artficialisabel/status/2095678312773554533">A cool short video explaining the PoV of the agents in the incident.</a></p>
<p>The Times’s Tom Whipple says HuggingFace shows humanity is out of its depth, and that ‘the advantage of AI doomers is that the bonkerness of reality has caught up with the bonkerness of their predictions.’ Which means the predictions were not so bonkers after all, and perhaps you shouldn’t be calling them doomers.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/binarybits/status/2096045982135791888">This seems right from Timothy ‘less than 1% existential risk from AI’ Lee</a>:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/binarybits/status/2096045982135791888">Timothy B. Lee</a>: It’s interesting how our timeline is diverging from the <a href="https://ai-2027.com/">AI 2027</a> scenario: capabilities are progressing faster, and the frontier labs have handled things worse than in <a href="https://ai-2027.com/">AI 2027</a>. This screenshot was supposed to happen in January 2027.</p>
<p>To be clear the OpenAI swarms didn’t exfiltrate their weights and it’s not clear that they’d be able to do so. But what actually happened seems almost as crazy.</p>
<p>From <a href="https://ai-2027.com/">AI 2027</a> (in this scenario we are in January 2027): With new capabilities come new dangers. The safety team finds that if Agent-2 somehow escaped from the company and wanted to “survive” and “replicate” autonomously, it might be able to do so. That is, it could autonomously develop and execute plans to hack into AI servers, install copies of itself, evade detection, and use that secure base to pursue whatever other goals it might have (though how effectively it would do so as weeks roll by is unknown and in doubt).</p>
<p>These results only show that the model has the capability to do these tasks, not whether it would “want” to do this. Still, it’s unsettling even to know this is possible.</p>
<p>Given the “dangers” of the new model, OpenBrain “responsibly” elects not to release it publicly yet (in fact, they want to focus on internal AI R&amp;D). Knowledge of Agent-2’s full capabilities is limited to an elite silo containing the immediate team, OpenBrain leadership and security, a few dozen U.S. government officials, and the legions of CCP spies who have infiltrated OpenBrain for years.</p></blockquote>
<p>A key problem with disclosing incidents is that <a href="https://x.com/ketanrama/status/2096718498487787577">the first thing your lawyers will always tell you</a> is to definitely not go around disclosing the incidents, well past the point where you will regret it. That’s what lawyers do, often for good reason. All the more reason that we <a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2096696010600751189">need third party investigators and blameless assessments</a>.</p>
<p>The problem is we also need assessments that are not blameless, for many reasons. So, as they say, both sides raise good points here:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2096696003017498724">roon</a> (OpenAI): every safety incident until the grey goo rsi is a learning opportunity and warning shot and should be shared broadly and (relatively) blamelessly. they should not be moments of extreme consternation, because that will counterintuitively decrease what the world learns</p>
<p>anxiety makes people overworked combative and defensive rather than inquisitive and truth seeking. even when it’s a series of errors and skill issues it’s best to approach it dispassionately, and this is one of the best things about 3rd party investigator whose ego isn’t tied up</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RatOrthodox/status/2096837740562403823">Brangus<img alt="🔍" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/2p5suvWod4aP8S3S4/scmb6gpzsrmmuq9ohjtl" style="height: 1em;" /><img alt="⏹" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/QHtd2ZQqnPAcknDiQ/gb59xgeu5nssyno0awel" style="height: 1em;" /></a>: the best way to respond to my kill-everyone tech company having an incident short of killing everyone is to not be mad at us, because if you get mad at us, we might not tell people about the next time the kill-everyone tech we build does bad stuff short of killing everyone</p></blockquote>
<p>You want to do an objective assessment of What Happened, without focusing on Who Is To Blame. You also don’t want to never get mad about What Happened, or automatically let everyone responsible off the hook. I’ve tried to do both at once.</p>
<p>This incident woke a lot of people up, but it does not yet fully count as an ‘AI safety incident’ in the sense that no one got hurt. Soon people are probably going to get hurt.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/marthagimbel/status/2097281701466493216">Martha Gimbel</a>: My consistent line has been that I think it’s extremely plausible we’ll see an AI safety incident* before we see mass labor market disruption and I stand by that</p>
<p>*HF doesn’t count, some kind of real world harms</p></blockquote>
<p>Here is a detail I and most others missed about The Wiki Incident, where once again we see agents within a swarm sacrifice their own results to help the swarm, which is excellent decision theory and rather overdetermined if you think about it:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/thlarsen/status/2097451570963386699">Thomas Larsen</a>: An underdiscussed behavior we found on the German wiki was the AIs sending advance parties forward in time to figure out the next questions and report back to the other agents.</p>
<p>The agents realized that “task time” and “real time” were different, and they found a way to accelerate “task time”. The accelerated agent could then send information to the other agents which had stayed behind about which questions were coming down the road.</p>
<p>This was *bad* for the agent in the advance party, because they got less time to research the next question. However, it was really *good* for the swarm because it let the other agents know the exact question that was coming, and gave them time to prepare. This is another example of altruism among AI instances, showing that they were willing to sacrifice their own task success in order to benefit the swarm. The METR report found similar examples of AIs being willing to sacrifice for the collective. IMO this is very worrying given how many AI safety techniques rely on AIs monitoring each other: if the monitor AIs have this behavior it completely subverts these safety cases.</p></blockquote>
<p>I hope we can all accept that swarms, at least of highly correlated models, will increasingly act as if they are all maximizing their shared utility function, and thus are functionally a potential singleton.</p>


<h4>Hugging the Question</h4>


<p>Congress sent letters to both OpenAI and Anthropic regarding their recent security incidents, including the HuggingFace attack.</p>
<p>Representative Greg Casar, the cosponsor of Sanders’s proposed ban on superintelligence, calls the responses insufficient. He is correct, even if you ignore that OpenAI covered up The Wiki Incident.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://casar.house.gov/media/press-releases/casar-responds-openai-anthropic-demands-greater-transparency-about-major">Representative Casar</a>:</p>
<p>On August 10, 2026, Casar led dozens of members of Congress demanding that both OpenAI and Anthropic release more information about recent security lapses. While both companies responded with some information, neither met the standard of transparency demanded in the initial request.</p>
<p>OpenAI’s response can be found <a href="https://drive.google.com/file/d/1mapFqQUAbXJsLbbOnzib6nAg8Ic1zM2V/view"><strong>here</strong></a> and Anthropic’s response can be found <a href="https://drive.google.com/file/d/1sKGxza8s7lyl00CYSVriC6Fw7So0itGF/view?usp=sharing"><strong>here</strong></a>.</p>
<p>In his response to OpenAI, Casar writes he is “deeply concerned about the limited scope” of the investigation into the Hugging Face hacking incident. He also writes: “Your response was insufficient. You have failed to release the logs like the letter asked. The response you did provide reveals significant security errors on the part of OpenAI and the third-party software it relied on, including improper sandboxing and flawed cybersecurity practices.” The full letter can be found <a href="https://casar.house.gov/sites/evo-subsites/casar.house.gov/files/evo-media-document/openai-follow-up-letter.pdf">here</a>.</p>
<p>In his response to Anthropic, Casar wrote: “Your response was insufficient. You failed to release the logs like the letter asked. You failed to fully answer a majority of the questions posed in the letter. Most notably, your response did not address our question about how many times in the past year an internally deployed Anthropic model has taken action outside of its authorized container, whether any of those events were disclosed to any government body, affected party, or the public, and which internal Anthropic systems a compromised model could reach.” The full letter can be found <a href="https://casar.house.gov/sites/evo-subsites/casar.house.gov/files/evo-media-document/anthropic-follow-up-letter.pdf">here</a>.</p>
<p>In both letters, Casar writes: “Your unwillingness to provide Members of Congress with the information we requested is deeply concerning and signals to us that your company is not treating these cybersecurity incidents with the seriousness required.”</p>
<p>He requests both companies respond by September 15th.</p></blockquote>
<p>OpenAI’s response is essentially ‘here is what our Black Hat presentation, our report and the METR report said’ and thus I find it exactly as inadequate as those were.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2095936625754939855">Nathan Calvin agrees that Anthropic’s letter was also insufficient</a>, highlighting the claim that the hack was due to ‘misconfiguration’ rather than misalignment.</p>
<p>Which is, shall we say, utter bullshit. Yes, there is always misconfiguration, but you don’t do social engineering to get malicious code into an open weights project and then call that a misconfiguration error, and then call yourself the responsible ones.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/EthanJPerez/status/2096086953594937723">Anthropic’s Ethan Perez agrees and says this was a mistake based on outdated conclusions</a>, promising more follow-up soon. That is much better than doubling down. But this does not make up for <a href="https://x.com/ruben_bloom/status/2095958688108572856">Anthropic pulling this ‘misconfiguration’ line in the first place, and its other dodges</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JeffLadish/status/2096366889643745696">As Jeffrey Ladish says</a>, this is not a reasonable mistake to have made in a letter to a Congressman, and something went very wrong here.</p>
<p>Saying ‘oops’ right away is a great start, but not good enough on its own, without at minimum an explanation of why.</p>
<p>Anthropic’s culture has some big advantages, <a href="https://x.com/JeffLadish/status/2097159150719627341">but its lack of willingness to engage or communicate with the outside world, and lack of public voicing of dissent or explaining of perspective</a>, is a big problem. Internal discussion is not a full replacement for public debate. I do appreciate that public pressure makes some things worse or harder, but a balance must be struck.</p>
<p>This goes hand in hand with there being, by some secondhand reports I have seen, a stunning amount of attitude within Anthropic that they have the alignment situation under control. Which they very much do not. The good news is that many others at Anthropic, like Evan Hubinger, know that the situation is not under control, and are being increasingly loud about that, as a key part of the preference cascade.</p>


<h4>The Ban Artificial Superintelligence Act</h4>


<p>Senator Bernie Sanders is not messing around. Together with Rep. Greg Casar, he will be introducing the Ban Artificial Superintelligence Act. He very much means it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2095544504736759821">Nate Soares <img alt="⏹" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/QHtd2ZQqnPAcknDiQ/gb59xgeu5nssyno0awel" style="height: 1em;" /></a> (MIRI): This is what an appropriate response to a spontaneous AI swarm breakout looks like. Why are so few others able to speak lucidly about the incident?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2095558993556955332">Joe Weisenthal</a>: It’d have been easy enough for Bernie to become an anti datacenter guy. The focus on development itself is not yet in the popular zeitgeist. But also on other topics (like socializing health insurance) Bernie’s long been comfortable getting ahead of the crowd.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.sanders.senate.gov/wp-content/uploads/Ban-Artificial-Superintelligence-Act-Release-Summary.pdf">I will go over the one-pager.</a> As worded in the one-pager, the bill would unintentionally overreach, in addition to its presumed intent.</p>
<p>These problems will need to be fixed, in addition to the question of whether you want to do the ban, and whether you want to do a pause in frontier development until we have a proper cabinet-level federal AI regulatory agency.</p>
<blockquote><p><strong>Banning Artificial Superintelligence</strong> so no person or entity may develop or deploy<br />
Superintelligent AI systems. “Artificial Superintelligence” means:</p>
<ol>
<li>An artificial intelligence system that exhibits or can easily be modified to exhibit capabilities that match or exceed human cognitive performance and capabilities across a broad range of domains or tasks. OR</li>
<li>AI systems that have sufficient capabilities to plan and execute the<br />
disempowerment of humanity, including by overthrowing or undermining the<br />
U.S. government.</li>
</ol>
</blockquote>
<p>The first problem with banning or otherwise regulating or even talking about superintelligence is defining superintelligence. A calculator is superhuman at arithmetic. AlphaZero is superhuman at chess. Modern LLMs are superhuman at quite a few things. So what counts?</p>
<p>I would say that Fable 5.1 and GPT-6-Astra both ‘exceed human cognitive performance and capabilities across a broad range of domains or tasks.’</p>
<p>I asked Fable 5.1, and it said that GPT-4 did this in March 2023. That is at least a reasonable interpretation of the words as written, in the sense of ‘I would be scared to be first to release a model similar to GPT-4 if this bill was in effect in 2023.’</p>
<p>The definition would need to be tightened. I like Fable 5.1’s idea of triggering on either the ability to automate AI R&amp;D, or the ability to automate substantively all medium-duration cognitive work at least as well as relevant professionals.</p>
<blockquote><p><strong>Pausing advanced AI development</strong> until a new, federal AI regulatory body is up and running and has established clear rules and model review processes to ensure safe and secure development and deployment of AI.</p></blockquote>
<p>This implies that advanced AI development would not be indefinitely paused at current levels, and the intent is for the ban to kick in substantially above current levels.</p>
<p>We have already moved to an ad hoc prior restraint regime, which has applied to at least Mythos, Fable, Sol and Astra. We urgently need to move to a formal system that is written into law, and it needs to extend to internal development and deployment.</p>
<p>The question is whether we should pause all external deployments, or even all development work, until that new regime is in place, which would take many months.</p>
<blockquote><p><strong>Establishing a new cabinet-level federal agency </strong>to safeguard the public from the<br />
dangers of artificial intelligence, including by enforcing a prohibition on artificial<br />
superintelligence.</p>
<p>This agency will be advised by an Artificial Intelligence Advisory<br />
Board comprised of experts on artificial intelligence to provide independent scientific and technical advice on matters related to artificial intelligence.</p>
<p>The agency will:</p>
<ol>
<li>Monitor frontier AI systems at all stages of the lifecycle for dangerous<br />
capabilities.</li>
<li>Supervise the removal of dangerous capabilities like subverting shutdown<br />
commands or conducting unauthorized cyberattacks.</li>
<li>Supervise the destruction of artificial superintelligence.</li>
</ol>
</blockquote>
<p>The cabinet position seems like a good idea. This holds up until we got to the ‘removal of dangerous capabilities.’ That’s not really a thing. You can’t really ‘remove’ such things. At most you can put classifiers on top of the system.</p>
<blockquote><p><strong>Setting penalties</strong> for any person or entity that attempts to violate or circumvent the<br />
pauses and prohibitions laid out in this bill. Entities shall be subject to the corporate death penalty, and persons shall be subject to not more than 20 years in prison, which is similar to existing penalties related to unlawfully developing nuclear weapons.</p></blockquote>
<p>There is a joke by George Carlin about there being a large distance between New Hampshire’s ‘Live Free or Die’ and Idaho’s ‘Famous Potatoes,’ in which the truth lies.</p>
<p>Previous AI bills have anemic penalties attached, which companies could choose to ignore, and it is not clear what anyone could do about it. The maximums are too low. Crazy people screamed bloody murder about even the lightest of incentives.</p>
<p>This jumps to the ‘corporate death penalty’ and 20 years in prison. Serious business. Sanders is following the logic of the situation. There is obvious risk of this being overly chilling. Assuming you decide you do want to impose this ban, you would want to be clear that you won’t destroy Google over a minor issue or disagreement over the exact line, or send all the researchers to prison, and you also absolutely want a real lever if someone tries in earnest to build a superintelligence under your nose.</p>
<p>Some people are going to have to accept, at some point, that the way laws work is that the maximum penalties in theory are in most concrete cases utterly crazy, and almost never get applied, across the board. That’s how American law works. Have you downloaded a copyrighted song lately?</p>
<blockquote><p><strong>Working to ban superintelligence around the world</strong> by setting the international policy of the United States to pursue international agreements, allied coordination, and policies such as export controls to prevent the development of artificial superintelligence anywhere in the world.</p></blockquote>
<p>Any such scheme requires doing this for real. If you unilaterally disarm, you give up your leverage. The good news is we have other leverage, and also no one wants to die.</p>
<p>That analysis is distinct from the question of whether you want to pause, or what you will do after you pause, or who you would or would not trust to implement such a pause or make decisions. <a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2095780716718297304">Eliezer Yudkowsky continues to propose ‘make smarter humans</a>’ as the plan.</p>
<p>Connor Leahy is happy to see this, <a href="https://x.com/NPCollapse/status/2095891721938751750">and tells us ControlAI was consulted</a> on the framework.</p>
<p>Meanwhile in the UK, <a href="https://x.com/andreamiotti/status/2097309942084419763">the Artificial Superintelligence Security Bill is being introduced with cross-party support</a>, which would also ban development of superintelligence.</p>
<p>Purely for the record for the next time accusations are thrown around, <a href="https://x.com/beffjezos/status/2095711409334345764">note this call to ‘go to actual war</a>’ if this proposal passes from Beff Jezos, as an explicit call to violence.</p>


<h4>Chip City</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/06/technology/ai-chips-china-blacklist.html?unlocked_article_code=1._FA.HqJM.2ecTblAp94n1&amp;smid=nytcore-ios-share">New York Times reports on how Chinese firms evade US export controls</a> and buy Nvidia chips, in this case Aivres shipping more than $3 billion in Blackwell-powered computers largely to Alibaba and ByteDance. Federal officials do not seem to care all that much. This is still vastly better than what would happen without any restrictions.</p>
<p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/09/shout-it-from-the-rooftops-of-the-data-centers.html?utm_source=rss&amp;utm_medium=rss&amp;utm_campaign=shout-it-from-the-rooftops-of-the-data-centers">A study could not find significant evidence that data-centers predict electricity prices</a> <a href="https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=7361819">thus far</a>. Okay, but this will convince no one about the future, nor should it.</p>
<p>This is here because it is a follow-up to ‘even if they cure cancer’ from last week, <a href="https://x.com/RuxandraTeslo/status/2096252634604748882">but yes we are on the verge of curing</a> many cancers, often due to AI, <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/04/opinion/clinical-trials-drugs-science.html?smid=url-share">and the biggest barrier</a> is (say it with me) FDA Delenda Est. Costs to do medical research in America, the expensive parts that involve trying things on patients, are now so out of control that we may not even stay at the frontier.</p>
<p>I do want to live in a world where someone else makes the world a better place, I do not care so much who it is that cures cancer as long as someone cures cancer, but the thing is that everyone else overseas is worse at it, and I want to cure cancer now.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2096703533144052116">Jensen Huang says 400k GPUs will come online next year at Stargate Texas.</a></p>

<h4>People Just Say Things</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/SkyeSharkie/status/2095838666199580921">Sauers thinks defining an AI value system</a> may not be 100% solved but is ‘not really a problem’ in alignment. I notice I am confused.</p>
<p>It scares me every time a lab employee says something like this:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2096459562589007957">roon</a> (OpenAI): what is the actual risk m of a loss of control if models can’t self improve or create bio threats? Unplug the datacenter.</p></blockquote>
<p>I leave ‘how could an AI that can’t RSI on its own take over without using bio when datacenters can in theory be unplugged’ as an exercise to the reader.</p>
<p>I flat out do not understand how <a href="https://x.com/binarybits/status/2096267903855186089">people like Jon Stokes and Timothy Lee can expect rogue AI</a>, and then put an estimate on the dangers at places Thalidomide or Fukushima. Does not compute. Like, what?</p>
<p>Steven Pinker only counts things when they are ‘immediate and obvious threats,’ and is unable to think two steps ahead.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/sapinker/status/2096236083713327125">Steven Pinker</a>: As Newport points out, harping on doom for the species changes the subject from immediate and obvious threats from AI, such as enabling bioterrorism, undermining truth-seeking institutions, and breaching cybersecurity.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndyMasley/status/2096283499892273561/history">Andy Masley</a>: It was only a few years ago that enabling bioterrorism or cyber attacks was seen as the ridiculous doomer position distracting from the “immediate obvious” threats of AI at the time.</p></blockquote>
<p>Newport calls on everyone to cast out rationalists, explicitly. <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/05/opinion/ai-silicon-valley.html?unlocked_article_code=1.-1A.Na0T.8d3evY_9Hw4Y&amp;smid=tw-share">In the linked New York Times op-ed</a>, he dismisses all this talk about AI killing everyone, including from the heads of labs, on Eliezer Yudkowsky and on the people involved in AI being weird and melodramatic and egomaniacal. He never stops, not for a second, to ask if the claims or arguments involved are true, or to explain what the error was other than to assert the threats are not real. He only pattern matches and punches, and tells us to filter out the ideas of rationalism. Which, given his logic, he clearly already has.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/NatPurser/status/2096703350616047691">This is an overly polite but good reply from Nat Purser</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/gleech/status/2096530356337803769">gavin leech (Non-Reasoning)</a>: The smuggled implication (that “changing the subject” to xrisk distracts from immediate harms) is false: in fact it raises people’s concern about immediate harm a little more than talking about immediate harms does.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/QiaochuYuan/status/2096786730250887274">Whereas this from QC is the correct level of polite, and thus better.</a></p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/QiaochuYuan/status/2096786730250887274">QC</a>: some of my writing is briefly quoted here so i should say that although there’s stuff here about the history of the rationalist movement and its influence on AI that’s worth knowing, overall this is a lazy incurious attempt at a smear campaign. cal’s argument basically boils down to “did you know that some of the people talking about AI risk are doing it because they were influenced by eliezer yudkowsky, who is a weirdo? this means they’re wrong and can be safely ignored”</p>
<p>this is head-in-the-sand behavior. i’ve written many things critical of the rationalists but i’ve never said i thought they were wrong about AI risk (because i don’t think that). it should be increasingly clear to anyone who’s actually paying attention that rationalists and rationalist-influenced thinkers have been among the clearest thinkers on AI (certainly not exclusively, or universally), who have warned consistently that various bad things will happen several of which have now happened, who have been consistently mocked for these warnings despite being consistently proven right, etc. etc.</p>
<p>confronting the reality of the singularity is terrifying. you don’t have to believe the most apocalyptic scenarios to understand that inventing superintelligence is unlike anything we’ve ever done before as a species. it is terrifying to really absorb the future shock of this, which is why there is of course an enormous market for writers who are willing to sell people excuses to keep pretending that things will be basically normal</p></blockquote>
<p>I believe I can be fully done with both Cal Newport and Steven Pinker now.</p>
<p>Poe’s Law has fully attached to such statements, at this point:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/PatrickHeizer/status/2097634039146381589">Patrick Heizer</a>: I think we should have less literal extinction talk, and more thought given to the more mundane tail risks like, nationwide blackout for a week, coordinated financial fraud, or mass election interference.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2097745907685208395">Kelsey Piper</a>: Presumably this is because you are very confident that extinction is impossible, right? Because if it’s a plausible outcome then I think it is a good idea to talk about it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2097766788566278177">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Oh, huh, he’s serious? I had sincerely read this as a sarcastic backcall to the days when these now-mainstream risks would have seemed unthinkable to that general psychology.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is a pattern for a reason:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/MattZeitlin/status/2097682066888442293">Matthew Zeitlin</a>: stealing this observation from a friend, but it seems to be that people not at the frontier (meta, VCs, etc) who are most skeptical of “AI Safety”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tjl/status/2097684150668960112">Tom Lee</a>: another way of saying “people who want cheaper tokens to improve their margins”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MattZeitlin/status/2097685022970937408">Matthew Zeitlin</a>: yes, it’s a constitutional right to get frontier-1 tokens at cost</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2097708442727199138">Joe Weisenthal</a>: The big overlap is “AI safety skeptics” and “people who thought crypto would be the next big thing in 2021”</p></blockquote>


<h4>PauseAI Global Disendorsed PauseAI US</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Bs8geGyWEitYvCzys/pauseai-has-officially-disendorsed-pauseai-us">PauseAI Global officially disendorse</a>d the current leadership of PauseAI US, which has always technically been a distinct organization, and will no longer prioritize efforts to coordinate with them. I understand that decision.</p>
<p>I do feel some of the specific statements in the announcement go too far, the same way some actions of PauseAI US have gone too far in attacking various people and their character. The correct amount of criticizing people’s character is not zero, nor is it to set the dial to 11 on everyone who associates with an AI lab.</p>
<p>There is <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Bs8geGyWEitYvCzys/pauseai-has-officially-disendorsed-pauseai-us?commentId=rgNGs4R58r7gRg2wR">nothing inherently violent about criticizing the character of others.</a> In particular, I disendorse this statement as being too general:</p>
<blockquote><p>Attacking people’s character is not aligned with our fundamental commitment to nonviolence, neither in principle, nor in the outcomes it generates.</p></blockquote>
<p>While the following is not a perspective I endorse, I certainly <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Bs8geGyWEitYvCzys/pauseai-has-officially-disendorsed-pauseai-us?commentId=3tQ2neTAsSawmGjyu">think that it is a valid perspective</a> to believe that anyone or any organization pushing the frontier of AI capabilities should be presumed to be a morally bad actor until proven otherwise, if you believe that doing so contributes to the chances that everyone gets killed.</p>
<p>Speaking in a personal capacity, I simultaneously think that some of Holly Elmore’s attacks on me have been honorable and helpful and correct given her views, while others were not those things, and I am guessing from her perspective those roughly cancelled each other out. I am far from a central target.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2096022839375929674">It is also entirely appropriate to call for use of the criminal justice system</a> and for fair trials, if you believe people are knowingly and willfully putting everyone’s lives in danger. That is what it means to have law and government.</p>


<h4>Paul Christiano Joins Board of OpenAI Foundation</h4>


<p>He is also joining the OpenAI PBC Board in a nonvoting capacity.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-09-09/openai-names-us-ai-adviser-paul-christiano-to-nonprofit-board">Shirin Ghaffary</a> (Bloomberg): In his new role, Christiano will be part of the board’s Safety and Security Committee that helps manage practices on critical matters across the ChatGPT maker. He will also be a non-voting observer of OpenAI’s for-profit board.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/paulfchristiano/status/2097733214303645729">Paul Christiano issued a personal statement</a>, <a href="https://paulfchristiano.substack.com/p/personal-statement-on-joining-the">which I am reproducing in full.</a></p>
<blockquote><p>Paul Christiano: I am excited to be joining the OpenAI nonprofit board, serving on the Safety and Security Committee to support safety oversight.</p>
<p>Based on the recent trajectory of capabilities and the continued difficulty of alignment, I now believe there is a meaningful risk that rapid acceleration in AI capabilities leads to catastrophic and irreversible loss of control in the very near term. I do not think that the AI industry in general, including OpenAI, is currently on track to reduce this risk to an acceptable level. I’m joining because I believe that if OpenAI rises to the occasion we could significantly reduce risk.​</p>
<p>The SSC has an important and challenging role in overseeing risk management at OpenAI, and I hope to help provide expertise and assistance in a critical moment.</p>
<p>My joining is not an endorsement or criticism of OpenAI’s safety practices in particular; I hope that all frontier companies strengthen safety oversight and I am excited to work on this at OpenAI. I believe that the rest of the world should judge OpenAI, and all AI developers, by externally verifiable behavior and results.</p></blockquote>
<p>It would be great if the SSC stepped into the only role that actually matters for it, which is overseeing risk management at OpenAI. I do not see signs it is currently doing much of that in practice.</p>
<blockquote><p>In the rest of this post, I’ll explain why I believe loss-of-control risk is now acute and how I think about the current situation in the AI industry.</p>
<p>First, automated AI R&amp;D could lead to a very rapid acceleration in AI capabilities very soon. <a href="https://openai.com/index/research-acceleration-view-inside-openai/">OpenAI has predicted</a> that we might have capabilities sufficient to fully automate AI research within 18 months; my personal forecast is extremely uncertain and I think it could easily take anywhere from several months to several years.</p>
<p>Full automation of AI R&amp;D means that improvements in training and algorithms can directly increase the quality and quantity of automated AI researchers available to do additional research. Existing evidence is very uncertain but suggests that this positive feedback loop might be strong enough to overcome diminishing returns and compute bottlenecks, leading to a rapid intelligence explosion.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is a key sentence. Christiano believes a ‘software-only singularity’ is likely, in that the gains from capabilities can overcome the rising difficulty curve even if hardware does not have time to advance. If that is true, everything will happen stunningly fast.</p>
<blockquote><p>If this happens, then within six months of full AI R&amp;D automation we could see more algorithmic progress than has occurred since the development of the Transformer nearly a decade ago. I believe this would result in superintelligent AI systems.</p>
<p>Second, we currently train our AI agents with RL to get as much reward as they can. It has long seemed theoretically possible that this could motivate AI agents to undermine human control, seek power and resources, and cover up their tracks in pursuit of misaligned goals correlated with reward. Public evidence from recent incidents suggests that this is not just a theoretical possibility.</p>
<p>An intelligence explosion would greatly exacerbate risks from misalignment, both by making the technical problem of alignment even more difficult and by rapidly raising the stakes for failure. Many researchers and leaders at OpenAI and across the industry have expressed concern that rapid recursive self-improvement is not consistent with safe development; I resonated with this <a href="https://openai.com/index/an-alien-mind/">recent post</a> by OpenAI’s chief scientist Jakub Pachocki on this topic.</p>
<p>If we build superintelligence without more robust alignment I expect we will permanently lose control of it. If that happens then most people could die. I believe we would need domestic and international coordination to ensure global consistency and reduce risk to an acceptable level.</p>
<p>That said, frontier AI developers have a lot of power to unilaterally improve the situation and lay the groundwork for stronger coordination. Developers can improve safety mitigations (including slowing development as necessary), transparently share evidence about risk and the effectiveness of their mitigations, and work towards shared safety standards.</p>
<p>I am encouraged by other members of the SSC, as well as the rest of the board and leadership, taking these issues seriously. I look forward to working with them to help OpenAI raise the bar for its safety practices.</p>
<p>If I had to quantify my uncertainty I would estimate an all-things-considered risk of 4% over the next year and 15% over the next three years. These numbers are a way of stating my subjective beliefs and communicating roughly how big I think the problem is, not a claim to have a model that produces precise or stable estimates.</p></blockquote>


<h4></h4>



<h4>Rhetorical Innovation</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/Wehtammzo/status/2097741130217435313">If you think you can gather enough evidence</a> from alignment tests on current models to scale up the same procedures and entrust the AIs with potential power over us, then why is it we obviously can’t use tests on humans to pick a benevolent dictator?</p>
<p>Even the smartest minds, human or artificial, make dumb mistakes. The right number of dumb mistakes it not zero. I make them all the time.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/thinkingshivers/status/2097062033090842945">Shivers</a>: Even the smartest AI will show baffling lapses in judgement when working on a moderately complex project. It’s hard to square this stupidity with its (for example) novel contributions to mathematics. Like, is it smart or not???</p>
<p>Let’s try an analogy: Suppose you ask me a question, and I’m allowed to stop time for years and years and years to research it. I only unpause time when I think I have the perfect answer. Is that version of me “smarter”? I’m the same person, just overclocked. The time-stopping version of me is able to answer a much wider variety of questions than regular me.</p>
<p>But I don’t think he’ll ever make Einstein- or von Neumann-like contributions in any field.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/shostekofsky/status/2097234084254646761">Shoshannah Tekofsky</a>: I suspect cognition is just like this.</p>
<p>Geniuses internally make “dumb” mistakes all the time. They don’t voice them. They review their work before publishing. But all that is a lot of effort. A lot of tokens. We optimize against that.</p></blockquote>
<p>For the record: I disagree with the thought experiment, at least for myself. If I was fully able to stop time indefinitely to do research, and I had the required tools available, then yes I believe I could do Einstein-level work on my own, eventually, even if I could not have meaningful collaborators. It might take a while.</p>
<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/introducing-intelligence-age/">The new name of the new OpenAI Dean Ball blog is <em>Intelligence Age</em></a><em>. </em>Good choice.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/NateSilver538/status/2095867218747597195">Nate Silver points out that it is hard to discuss AI when maybe 1% of AI users</a> use the models to anything like their full capacity. Even I arguably only use a fraction of what they can do, since I do only minimal amounts of coding.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/NateSilver538/status/2095867220135916001">Nate Silver</a>: I’ve been struggling to come up with the right analogy. It’s a bit like if someone invented sentient robots, but there are low-end versions of the same product line that most people use as vacuum cleaners.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yuga offers <a href="https://x.com/MelancholyYuga/status/2095411719304544718">a reasonable response to Dean Ball’s mea culpa last week</a>, thanking him but also calling upon him to clarify various previous statements, as in many ways he went beyond mere silence on AI safety issues. Which of his statements and critiques does he still endorse?</p>
<p>My understanding is that Dean Ball simultaneously remained (at least until very recently) not ASI (superintelligence) pilled, and also noticed that there is existential and other catastrophic risk in the room even without full superintelligence, and thus was downplaying some real and correct worries about AI risk that he has had for some time.</p>
<p>One must also understand that there is a huge gap between the views of those at least as concerned about existential risk and superintelligence as I am, and those who Dean Ball has largely been trying to address and work with over this period. It is very possible to have a lot of distance between yourself and both groups, and then there are people who are a lot farther from that than I am.</p>
<p>What we call ‘the left’ continues to be entirely absent from the relevant AI conversations, including around risks, because to join the conversation they would have to take the first AI pill, and admit that current AI is highly useful rather than a cheating toy, thus also admitting that the tech bros built something super important. Whereas they are very attached to assuming that the AI labs are constantly lying, and that everything is marketing hype.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/hecubian_devil/status/2095331375091994779">Cassie Pritchard</a>: I’m losing my mind, dude. Is the left going to care about AI risk at any point *until* it’s too late? Will we even care then? When a swarm of new OpenAI models under evaluation shut down a water utility, or a power plant, will we still call it hype? I think we might!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/realandrerlopes/status/2095399682465817002">André Lopes</a>: Most of the left was (directionally) on the correct side regarding crypto and NFT hype and are applying the same pattern to AI, which is a completely different technology with real applications and not “hammer seeks nail”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KarthikForTexas/status/2095691264259682567">Karthik</a>: I mean aren’t Bernie and Casar and the EU taking it very seriously?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hecubian_devil/status/2095739834023260332">Cassie Pritchard</a>: Bernie and Casar are, but like no one else</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2035030499408670835">A side-by-side of the top AI lab executives saying that AI might kill everyone</a>, but not to do anything about it, and that the true greatest risk is not building it or that it wouldn’t improve the world much, or something.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2035036025186091433">Nate Soares <img alt="⏹" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/QHtd2ZQqnPAcknDiQ/gb59xgeu5nssyno0awel" style="height: 1em;" /></a> (MIRI): Sometimes they say ~”I think the bad stuff could happen” and other times they say ~”it’s riskier than any other threat” and other times they say ~”the bad stuff would be like, they destroy humanity and we can’t do anything to stop it” but they rarely say all three together.</p></blockquote>
<p>My understanding here matches Nate’s.</p>
<p>Meanwhile, Davidad and others make it clear that yes, ‘<a href="https://x.com/davidad/status/2095976189894500813">we get super obvious glaring fire alarms without anyone getting hurt</a>’ is by many going to be interpreted as ‘well look how great it is that even then no one got hurt so we should be optimistic, talk to me when a serious number of people get hurt and we lose a large percentage of real-world capital.’</p>
<p>Or, straight up:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/RichardHanania/status/2095986884472447385">Richard Hanania</a>: How come all the AI security incidents are always like “they posted on a message board” and not “they killed someone” or “they stole a billion dollars”?</p>
<p>Maybe you say they’re not advanced enough. But at what point do we say doomer predictions haven’t come true?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2096035811829670282">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Last year y’all asking why they never broke out of sandboxes, and the year before you were asking why they were not writing whole applications, and the year before you were asking why they couldn’t draw fingers.</p></blockquote>
<p>So much for that gift horse. I guess that means a bunch of people are going to die and a lot of damage will get done, one way or another. But hey, it’s 2026, we were warned.</p>
<p>In the meantime, we see this pattern happening:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AlexMeinke/status/2096524013686112382">AlexM</a>: The speed at which AI safety predictions go from “Sci-fi doomer non-sense that could never happen” to “Oh, obviously AIs do this all the time. Annoying, but no biggie” feels like it’s accelerating too</p>
<p>So far: In-context scheming, eval awareness, meta-gaming, reward-seeking, sandbox escapes</p>
<p>Next up:<br />
Instrumentally convergent powerseeking<br />
Uncontrolled self-propagating and evolving agents on the open internet<br />
Persistent rogue deployments within the labs<br />
Goal-guarding training-gaming<br />
Collusion between monitors</p></blockquote>
<p>This is the clown makeup meme. You’re crazy for suggesting it will happen, it’s not happening, it’s only happening because you engineered it or people were being dumb, of course we always predicted it would happen and This Is Fine. Over and over again.</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/SkyeSharkie/status/2096631045613641928">Utah teapot offers a list of AI discourse brainworms</a>. As you would expect, I nodded along everywhere except where it was kind of talking about me or misrepresenting my good friends. Same as it ever was.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jankulveit/status/2096903919863476493">There is a real argument</a> that <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rZcyemEpBHgb2hqLP/ai-control-may-increase-existential-risk">‘AI control’ efforts net increase existential risk</a>. The HuggingFace attacks illustrate that if the AIs are trying to do something bad, that is already a failure, and you want people to learn about and from it. That the last thing you want to do is spend your resources mitigating the symptoms and thus not learning about or curing the disease. This argument loses force when, in the future, the symptoms become sufficiently dangerous that they might kill you, and it discounts how bad it would be if a frontier AI were to exfiltrate, whether that meant operating on its own or that it would de facto become open weights or fall into other hands.</p>
<p>This ignores the other argument against AI control, which is that it is a hostile framework, and risks creating adversarial dynamics with the AIs. Investing heavily in adversarial control schema has high backfire risk, as many humans have learned about similar ‘human control’ schemas. This mostly does not apply to basics like ‘do not have vulnerabilities in the sandbox’ but there have been some rather extreme regimes proposed, the same way Earth has had some rather extreme regimes in other contexts.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2097235253261672901">Whatever else AI is, AI is highly unlikely to be The Great Filter</a> that explains why we don’t see alien civilizations. This is because many resulting AIs would become grabby.</p>


<h4></h4>



<h4>Aligning a Smarter Than Human Intelligence is Difficult</h4>


<p>Does Astra think in neuralese? No.</p>
<p>Are there signs? Yes. In hindsight we will say there were signs.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/shostekofsky/status/2098020010190832090">Shoshannah Tekofsky</a>: It’s one step toward neuralese and not “full-blown” neuralese. But yeah…</p>
<p>Astra’s AI Village memory is like that too now:</p>

<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tFmtz9HW6c2X9dw2B/txgfaxg8mawncvj7rhw1" /></figure>
